Songdo, Incheon, Republic of Korea

Consideration of funding proposals -
Addendum X
Funding proposal package for FP235
Summary
This addendum contains the following seven parts:
a) A funding proposal titled “Mangroves for climate: Public, Private and Community
Partnerships for Mitigation and Adaptation in Ecuador”;
b) No-objection letter issued by the national designated authority(ies) or focal
point(s);
’
c) Environmental and social report(s) disclosure;
’
d) Secretariats assessment;
e) Independent Technical Advisory Panels assessment;
f) Response from the accredited entity to the independent Technical Advisory Panel's
assessment; and
g) Gender documentation.

Note to Accredited Entities on the use of the funding proposal template
• Accredited Entities should provide summary information in the proposal with cross-
reference to annexes such as feasibility studies, gender action plan, term sheet, etc.
• Accredited Entities should ensure that annexes provided are consistent with the details
provided in the funding proposal. Updates to the funding proposal and/or annexes must be
reflected in all relevant documents.
• The total number of pages for the funding proposal (excluding annexes) should not
exceed 60. Proposals exceeding the prescribed length will not be assessed within the usual
service standard time.
• The recommended font is Arial, size 11.
• Under the GCF Information Disclosure Policy, project and programme funding proposals
will be disclosed on the GCF website, simultaneous with the submission to the Board,
subject to the redaction of any information that may not be disclosed pursuant to the IDP.
Accredited Entities are asked to fill out information on disclosure in section G.4.
Please submit the completed proposal to:
fundingproposal@gcfund.org
Please use the following name convention for the file name:
“FP-[Accredited Entity Short Name]-[Country/Region]-[YYYY/MM/DD]”

=== PAGE 10 ===
GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0 | PAGE 3
A
Impacts of climate change5
Climate change is projected to create unprecedented changes in conditions along Ecuador´s coast, including
increases in sea level, extreme El Niño-Southern Oscillation events, higher intensity and variability of precipitation,
and increased atmospheric temperatures. The combination of these changes is expected to cause significant and
more frequent coastal flooding, which will damage local communities and economic activity. Recent flood events
have caused death, homelessness, increased disease risk and over US$1 billion of damages and economic losses
(EM-DAT, 2021)6. Mangroves serve as natural structural barriers that reduce coastal erosion and provide important
flood-control functions, by dissipating wave energy and storm damage. Current mangrove cover directly provides
flood protection for coastal populations and is estimated to prevent approximately US$250 million in losses annually
from flooding in Ecuador’s coastal region. Conversely, ongoing deforestation of mangroves in proximity to coastal
populations magnifies exposure to climate-related flood risks and other climate impacts. Nearly 25% of Ecuador’s
mangroves have been deforested since 1969 with conversion to shrimp farms as the largest driver. As of 2018,
there were 156,633 hectares (ha) of mangrove remaining in mainland Ecuador, with an average of 990 ha of primary
mangrove forests lost each year.
There are opportunities to consolidate recent mangrove conservation successes and for mangrove restoration that
would strengthen natural flood protection while also providing other ecosystem service co-benefits (especially
supporting artisanal fisheries). Ecuador’s mangroves have been identified as part of the world’s irrecoverable
carbon stock – stores of carbon in nature that are vulnerable to release from human activity but, if lost, could not
be restored by 2050 when the world must reach net-zero emissions to avoid the worst impacts of climate change
(Noon et al., 2021). Protecting these carbon stocks and harnessing the long-term ability of mangroves to sequester
carbon will provide important global climate change mitigation benefits.
Despite recent stabilization of net mangrove coverage, in large part due to one-off restoration efforts during 2008-
2012 and community-based mangrove management, mangrove areas are expected to be a net source of emissions
from lost biomass and soil carbon for decades to come (see Annex 2). This project aims to transform the situation
by coordinating public sector, private sector and community efforts to protect and restore mangroves, thereby
reducing mangrove loss, resulting in a reduction of 4.6 M tCO2e of greenhouse gas (GHG) emissions7, and
accelerating Ecuador’s mangrove recovery to maintain and enhance flood protection for 89,600 people8 from
annual flooding risk and enhance the livelihoods of 41,500 people to increase their resiliency.
Proposed interventions
The current paradigm underestimates climate risks and undervalues the ecosystem services provided by
mangroves, augmenting both exposure to climate risk and greenhouse gas emissions. By fully valuing the
adaptation and mitigation benefits of mangrove ecosystems and integrating these into governance and economic
frameworks across Ecuador’s coastal region, the project will shift this paradigm with an innovative approach that
will create public, private and community partnerships to reduce GHG emissions and improve the livelihoods of
some of the country's most vulnerable people.
If local communities are provided with knowledge and resources for mangrove management and livelihoods
development, and if the private sector and government actively collaborate on mangrove protection and restoration,
and the enabling environment for mangrove protection is strengthened, then coverage and quality of mangrove
ecosystems will be increased, resulting in reduced climate change impacts on vulnerable coastal populations,
increased economic resilience, and reduced GHG emissions because healthy and more extensive mangroves
reduce flood impacts and sequester carbon.
By integrating the participation of local communities, private sector businesses, Ministry of Environment, Water and
Ecological Transition (MAATE) and subnational governments, the project will deliver mutually reinforcing, multi-
scale actions across the three project components to address key barriers.
Component 1. Mangrove areas under effective and climate-adapted management increased, including
through community-based management (AUSCEMs) and protected areas (PAs) implementing climate
adaptation plans. This component focuses on engaging local communities to strengthen community management
of mangroves, restore mangroves, increase the area of mangroves under protection by local community
stakeholders, and improve community economic sustainability.
Component 2. The private sector becomes a transformational agent for change by reducing GHG emissions
and providing financial support to conserve and restore mangroves that increase climate resilience for
other coastal populations. This component focuses on engaging the private sector, particularly shrimp
aquaculture, to become transformational agents for change, reversing their previous role as an agent of mangrove

=== PAGE 11 ===
GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0 | PAGE 4
A
loss, by integrating climate-smart production practices to increase climate resilience, reduce pressures for
mangrove deforestation, and restore mangrove ecosystems, as well as catalyzing new sources of financing for
long-term sustainability of mangrove conservation. An endowment of US$4 million is requested from GCF to build
up a dedicated financial mechanism (the recently created Socio Manglar subaccount of the Socio Bosque Fund9)
to provide long-term support to communities managing mangroves through the Agreements for Sustainable Use
and Custody of the Mangrove Ecosystem (AUSCEM) model. The US$4 million will be held in an interest-bearing
account and the interest used to fund community incentive payments. Alongside this, CI and MAATE will seek
additional financial contributions to build up the capital of the investment account of the Socio Manglar subaccount.
These additional contributions for community management of mangroves will be sought from the private sector
during the project as part of innovative Government of Ecuador programs to encourage private investment in climate
and nature conservation initiatives10. The project will promote more sustainable shrimp production methods but will
not invest directly in shrimp farms. Investment on shrimp farms to implement more sustainable production methods
will be financed by the private sector and will not use GCF funds.
Component 3. Create the enabling conditions for sustaining reductions in mangrove deforestation and
increased mangrove restoration by strengthening governance, climate change adaptation strategies,
coastal management policies, and legal enforcement. This component focuses on creating the enabling
conditions to increase adaptation and mitigation, through improved governance and the generation of timely,
targeted information, which are the scaffolding supporting mangrove conservation, planning, and regulation.
The project will contribute to achieving the GCF’s Strategic Plan (2024-2027) through its impact on the following
four GCF ‘targeted results’ for climate mitigation and adaptation efforts.
1. T1 Readiness (GCF Strategic Priority on Country Programming): National Climate Plan implementation.
Ecuador will be directly supported to advance implementation of its Nationally Determined Contribution
(NDC) as set out in the national NDC implementation plan (which also currently serves as the
implementation plan for the new (2023) National Adaptation Plan regarding adaptation interventions). The
project will support Ecuador’s conditional GHG reduction goals and adaptation goals to increase the cover
of mangrove and expand the use of community-based management of mangroves through AUSCEM. The
project is expected to reduce national GHG emissions by 730,000 tCO2e over the project implementation
period and 4.6 MtCO2e over the project lifespan. The project will strengthen management of 70,000 ha of
mangroves under community management through establishing new areas under management and
strengthening existing ones as part of the Socio Bosque program (SBP)11. Ecuador’s current NDC goal is
to increase the forest area in the SBP by 127,500 ha, including mangroves. As part of strengthening
mangrove management, the project will establish an additional 10 community-held AUSCEMs covering
mangroves, in addition to strengthening the existing 59 community associations with AUSCEM
agreements. (Ecuador’s current NDC goal is to establish 30 new AUSCEM agreements covering mangrove
areas.)
2. T4 Food (GCF Strategic Priority on Food). Mangroves provide a range of ecosystem services that directly
and indirectly contribute to the livelihoods and food security of local people. In addition to the flood
protection services that provide important climate change adaptation benefits, mangroves serve as
important nurseries and breeding grounds for species of fish, shrimp, crabs and clams that support artisanal
and commercial/industrial fisheries. 41,500 people are expected to benefit from low-emission, climate
resilient fisheries management practices that underpin their livelihoods.
3. T5 Ecosystems (GCF Strategic Priority on Ecosystems). The project will contribute to the protection and
sustainable management of 156,633 ha of mangrove ecosystems. This will be achieved through a
combination of increasing the area under community management, strengthening PA planning to integrate
climate change adaptation, improved coastal land-use planning and strengthening the capacity of
government agencies and local government to enforce existing regulations designed to protect mangroves.

4. T6 Infrastructure (GCF Strategic Priority infrastructure target). The project adopts a nature-based
solutions approach to infrastructure because mangroves are expected to provide flood protection benefits
that reduce risks of coastal flooding and related damage. The maintenance and improvement of 156,633
ha of mangrove ecosystems will be through conservation, sustainable use and restoration activities.
5. T9 Adaptation and locally-led action (GCF Strategic Priority on Adaptation proposals). The project will
promote locally led adaptation action through community management of mangroves and action by the
private sector to reduce deforestation and support mangrove restoration. The project will also support
locally-led adaptation through the support to land-use planning processes included in Component 3 of the
project.
In addition to the project’s contributions towards advancing national climate change adaption and mitigation plans,
the project will also contribute to the aims of the Global Mangrove Alliance (GMA)12, which brings together a global
community of practitioners to halt mangrove loss, restore lost mangroves and secure mangrove protection. CI is a
founding member along with other international organizations and the project will contribute in two ways to the
GMA’s work:
1. The project will contribute to knowledge sharing through the GMA, especially related to mangrove
restoration and “blue carbon” information. One of the functions of GMA is as a knowledge hub to share
information between practitioners engaged in mangrove conservation and restoration. The mangrove
restoration approach is informed by GMA toolkits and best practice guides. Data collected under activity
3.1.1 will also be shared with the GMA as a matter of course (through the Mangrove Restoration Tracker
Tool and Global Mangrove Watch).
2. The project itself makes an important contribution to the ‘Mangrove Breakthrough’, an initiative launched
by the GMA and the UN Climate Change high level Champions during the UNFCCC COP27. This high-
level initiative aims to secure the future of 15 million ha of mangroves globally (through conservation,
restoration and securing long-term financing of mangrove protection). This project alone represents about
1% of this goal.

PROJECT/PROGRAMME INFORMATION
B.1. Climate context (max. 1000 words, approximately 2 pages)
B.1.1 Overview of Ecuador’s Coast (further detail and full references for citations are provided in Annex 2, the
Feasibility Study)
1. Ecuador’s complex coastal geography comprises 3,631 km of coastline, which is home to seven million people
and Ecuador’s largest city, Guayaquil. Mangroves extend along 36% of Ecuador’s continental coastlines and
play a central role in sustaining both export-oriented industries and local livelihoods by providing habitat to
commercial fish species and maintaining healthy coastal zones. The coast is where a significant portion of the
nation’s economic output is concentrated, much of which is directly dependent on coastal and marine resources,
especially shrimp and small-scale fisheries.
Figure 1. Ecuador’s coastal region: 5 provinces (with 35 municipalities) and main urban centers and
mangrove areas (in 2018). Data sources: Towns from IGM (2018), provinces boundaries from CONALI
(2016), mangroves from MAAE (2018).

PROJECT/PROGRAMME INFORMATION
B.1. Climate context (max. 1000 words, approximately 2 pages)
B.1.1 Overview of Ecuador’s Coast (further detail and full references for citations are provided in Annex 2, the
Feasibility Study)
1. Ecuador’s complex coastal geography comprises 3,631 km of coastline, which is home to seven million people
and Ecuador’s largest city, Guayaquil. Mangroves extend along 36% of Ecuador’s continental coastlines and
play a central role in sustaining both export-oriented industries and local livelihoods by providing habitat to
commercial fish species and maintaining healthy coastal zones. The coast is where a significant portion of the
nation’s economic output is concentrated, much of which is directly dependent on coastal and marine resources,
especially shrimp and small-scale fisheries.
Figure 1. Ecuador’s coastal region: 5 provinces (with 35 municipalities) and main urban centers and
mangrove areas (in 2018). Data sources: Towns from IGM (2018), provinces boundaries from CONALI
(2016), mangroves from MAAE (2018).

=== PAGE 14 ===
GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0 | PAGE 7
B
2. Shrimp: Ecuador is the world's second largest exporter of farmed shrimp, with approximately 174,000 ha of
shrimp ponds and annual exports of 1,060,000 MT worth US$6.653 billion (Cámara Nacional de Acuacultura,
2022)13. The volume of exports has more than doubled in the past 10 years. Production is dominated by small
and medium producers who constitute 97% of farms and 76% of the total area (GPS, 2020). Ecuador is
recognized in the market for slightly higher quality shrimp, due in part to lower production intensities, but there
is still substantial room for intensification and modernization. Demand for certified shrimp is strong in the North
American and European markets – while price premiums are not always present, environmental certification is
often seen as a requirement to access these markets.
3. Small scale fisheries: Based on the most detailed national data, there are 28,399 artisanal fishers in Ecuador,
organized into 420 associations (Vice Ministry of Aquaculture and Fisheries (VMAP), 2013), with an estimated
113,596 people directly or indirectly dependent on artisanal fishing14. These fishers have low incomes; in the
Gulf of Guayaquil, 74% of fishers and gatherers earn less than US$400 per month, and 37% have monthly
incomes less than US$200 (Herrera et al., 2017).
B.1.2 Current and projected climate change impacts in the coast of Ecuador (see Annex 2, Feasibility Study
for more information)
4. In the continental coastal region of Ecuador, the climate is strongly influenced by the ocean. During the rainy
season from December to May, the air temperatures are slightly higher due to the warming of the sea
(intensification of the Panama current). The dry season coincides with the intensification of the South Pacific
Anticyclone, which in turn strengthens the Humboldt Current and the cold waters in the region. In the north-
central region, rainfall exceeds 2,000 mm annually, and air temperatures are between 25°C and 39°C. Further
south, the coastal zone is under the influence of the Humboldt Current, so its precipitation values are between
300 and 1,000 mm per year, and the average temperatures are between 24°C and 26°C.
5. Climate change is projected to create significant changes in local environmental conditions along Ecuador's
coast including increases in sea level, increased intensity of El Niño-Southern Oscillation events,
increased intensity and variability of precipitation, and atmospheric temperatures, all of which will affect
Ecuador’s vulnerable populations and economy.
6. By 2100, mean sea level is projected to rise an additional 49-64 cm on Ecuador's coast15. An increase in mean
sea level will also be compounded by increased frequency of extreme sea level (ESL) events (storm surge and
tidal flooding), which are expected to occur annually by mid-century in low-lying coastal areas (IPCC, 2019).
Increases in sea level rise (SLR) and ESL are expected to increase the risks of coastal flooding and
consequently impact rural and urban populations, and aquaculture in coastal areas, especially when
compounded by periodic El Niño events.
7. El Niño-Southern Oscillation (ENSO) is a phase of a large-scale, recurring climate pattern associated with
above average sea surface temperatures in the eastern Pacific, which contributes to increased sea levels and
extreme rainfall events in Ecuador. El Niño events have already led to observed ocean warming and dramatic
SLR due to seawater expansion (Cai et al., 2014; Wang et al., 2019). During the 1997-98 season, sea surface
temperatures off the coast of Ecuador increased by 5°C above normal and sea level increased up to 42 cm
(CAF, 2000). In the equatorial Pacific region, sea levels can deviate from the global mean sea level by as much
as 40 cm because of El Niño (Walsh et al., 2012). ENSO may be considered a natural climatic driver, and the
effect of climate change on future ENSO dynamics is unclear although rainfall variability is very likely to increase
over the east-central tropical Pacific (IPCC, 2014; 2021). There are indications that extreme El Niño events may
increase in frequency due to climate change (McPhaden et al., 2020).
8. Between 1960 and2016, the average annual precipitation for the coast of Ecuador increased 33% (INAMHI,
from Ministerio del Ambiente del Ecuador, 2017). Some models project significant increases in annual
precipitation for coastal Ecuador (9-10% under RCP 4.5 by 2071-2100). Analysis focused on the Santa Elena
peninsula by García-Garizábal et al. (2017) documented increasing temperatures (0.038ºC/year) and
precipitation (0.196 mm/year) for the period 1982-2011 with modeling (ECHOG A2) predicting increases in
temperature (+2.7ºC) and precipitation (+8,2%) by 2100. Other analyses indicate that while total annual rainfall
is not expected to increase significantly, greater variability is projected, with heavier localized rains (up to 15
more days per year of extreme rainfall events) and periodic dry spells expected to increase (Ministerio del

14 Assuming family size of four people.
15 https://sealevel.nasa.gov/ipcc-ar6-sea-level-projection-tool The SLR range cited is based on projections for scenario SSP2-4.5 (lower
estimate) and SSP3-7.0 (higher estimate).

Ambiente de Ecuador, 2019; UNDP, 2013), increasing the risk of flooding and drought (Ministerio del Ambiente
del Ecuador, 2012).
9. From 1960-2016, average temperature for Ecuador´s coast increased 0.6°C, and an increase in atmospheric
temperature of 2.2°C-2.9°C is expected by 2100 under RCP 4.5 and RCP 8.5, respectively. Though outside the
scope of this project, these temperature increases may affect livelihoods from agricultural activities and increase
health risks in project areas adjacent to or outside of mangroves. This variable is included for reference
purposes but is not directly expected to impact flood risks, which are the climate change adaptation focus of
this project.
10. Analyses conducted for this project by the International Research Center on El Niño (CIIFEN) indicate that
dissolved oxygen, primary production and chlorophyll would have reductions in their values under projected
climate change scenarios (RCP 4.5 and 8.5). The hydrogen potential off the coast of Ecuador would be reduced,
showing signs of possible ocean acidification, which could impact fisheries and associated livelihoods.
Climate-related flooding events in Ecuador
11. Flood risks are a significant concern for Ecuador's coast and in the last 45 years, the coastal provinces have
suffered the highest degree of flooding in the country. Guayas has been the most affected, with more than 100
floods occurring where water levels exceeded the drainage canals designed to convey stormwater runoff and
thus inundated adjacent lands (Secretaría Nacional de Gestión de Riesgos (SNGR), 2018). Augmented sea
level, coupled with the exceptionally high precipitation and associated with El Niño in coastal Ecuador, limits
the discharge of river systems into the ocean, leading to inland flooding. This results in impacts to drainage
throughout the coastal zone, which are not fully captured in existing coastal and river flooding models. Under
current assessments, 39% of areas within 5 km of Ecuador’s coastline are already at high risk of annual flooding
(SNGR, 2018).
12. Flooding events registered since 1965 on the coast of Ecuador have affected over 1.8 million people and caused
over US$1.5 billion in damages and economic losses, of which 96% occurred during El Niño events (EM-DAT,
2021). During those flooding events, 97,031 people became homeless and 836 people were killed (EM-DAT,
2021; see Figure 6). Those numbers, even if considered the best available, are underestimated as only events
that have impacted more than 100 people, and have killed more than 10 people, are recorded.
13. Historical El Niño events provide a reference point for the scale of future climate-related damages for Ecuador’s
coast. The El Niño event in 2015 generated damage to infrastructure and economic activities associated with
agricultural production. Ecuador’s agricultural losses were estimated at US$3.5 million, with more than 2,000
small- and medium-sized enterprises and 27,000 people affected by landslides and flooding (CIIFEN, 2017).
Limited government preparation and response to previous El Niño events, in areas such as land-use planning,
management, and decision making, are indicative of inadequate adaptive capacity in the face of climate change.
14. Extreme El Niño events, related precipitation variability on regional scales and increases in sea level will likely
intensify (Pachauri et al., 2015). Combined with the expected increase in SLR due to climate change but
unrelated to ENSO events, flooding of coastal areas is expected to increase. Future El Niño events may become
more frequent and more intense (Magrin et al., 2007; Wang et al., 2019; McPhaden et al, 2020), but even an
El Niño of similar magnitude to historical events will affect larger areas and more people as its flooding impacts
are compounded by SLR. These conclusions are based on several models that project flood risks under diverse
future scenarios and assumptions (see Section B 1.6 below).
B.1.3 Mangroves in Ecuador
Mangrove Ecosystem
15. Ninety-eight percent (98%) of Ecuador’s mangroves are concentrated along the shores of four main estuaries
- Cayapas Mataje, Muisne Cojimíes, Guayas and Jambelí (Figure 2), which spread across eight municipalities
within the project area, with over 1,000 ha of mangrove each, in three provinces16. These municipalities are

people fishing and gathering shellfish from mangroves is likely more than double the number of registered members in associations
(Zambrano, N. pers. comm.). Total estimated beneficiary numbers are based on 4.04 family members dependent on each of these fishers

Importance of mangroves for livelihoods of Ecuador’s coastal populations
16. Mangroves provide many valuable ecosystem services that contribute to human well-being, including provision
of timber and fuelwood, breeding and nursery grounds for fisheries and protection from coastal flooding and
erosion. People in these mangrove areas, including both urban and rural dwellers, are highly reliant on fishing
and gathering shellfish. The total population dependent on mangrove fisheries is approximately 42,000 people,
most of whom live in the project area19. This number does not include the larger coastal open-water fisheries
and associated economic activity, which are indirectly dependent on mangroves´ ecological function as critical
nurseries for a variety of economically important fish species. It is estimated that the national market for fish
and seafood products generated by small-scale fisheries is approximately US$200 million per year (Monnier et
al., 2020).
Historical changes to the area of mangrove land cover
17. Mangroves have experienced significant losses in Ecuador, which has resulted in GHG emissions from the
trees’ lost biomass and through the release of soil carbon. The most extensive losses have been linked to the
conversion of the intertidal land to shrimp aquaculture, an industry that was first introduced in 1968 and quickly
led to large scale deforestation. This trend has been consistent over time, and shrimp farms continue to be the
prominent driver of mangrove loss, with shrimp ponds during 2008-2018 accounting for 51% of mangrove loss20
(CIIFEN-MAE, 2020). As of 2018 (the date of the last national forest cover assessment) there were 156,633 ha
of mangrove remaining in the four key estuaries of mainland Ecuador21.
18. Recent years have seen growth in new mangrove areas replacing other land uses, with a total net gain in
mangrove area of 15,258 ha from 2008-2018. Despite gross gains of 15,161 ha in mangrove coverage, there
is also significant ongoing gross loss of mangroves, totaling an area of 9,903 ha lost during 2008-2018, resulting
in the overall 5,258 ha net gain. The ongoing loss of mangroves in these areas is the result of illegal logging of
mangroves (mostly for shrimp ponds but also for agriculture and building), which continues to occur due to weak
application of environmental regulations and poor territorial planning for mangrove conservation by local
governments. Mangroves are one of the nine forest strata included in the Forest Reference Emission Level
(FREL) and referenced in the REDD+ Action Plan.
Community-based management of mangroves and effectiveness for mangrove conservation and
restoration
19. Strengthening community management of mangroves has been an important pillar of Ecuador’s approach to
mangrove conservation for twenty years. Strengthening the capacity of local mangrove users as a strategy for
climate change adaptation has been highlighted by local studies and is an important priority in Ecuador’s climate
change strategies (see D.5).
20. While coastal mangroves are legally public lands, AUSCEM22, first established in 2000, grant traditional
community users formal rights linked to conservation commitments, allowing them to benefit from improved
livelihoods activities and living conditions. These agreements grant use rights and stewardship responsibilities

to organized groups of traditional mangrove users to restrict open access and overuse, reduce deforestation
and illegal extraction, and increase the ability of local users to realize economic benefits from conservation
actions. Community control and participation in conservation of mangrove areas, as well as national PAs, have
proven effective tools for mangrove protection. Gross deforestation rates under AUSCEMs and PAs are 86%
lower than areas without these protections; while mangrove areas without community stewardship or PA status
(totaling 36,186 ha or 23% of total mangrove area) account for 69% of all recent mangrove deforestation. The
coverage of mangrove areas under community-based management is, however, still insufficient. AUSCEMs
currently only cover 60,217 ha of mangroves (Figure 3 and Table 2), this means that significant mangrove areas
and their traditional users are not covered by active AUSCEMS, leaving them at risk of encroachment and
uncontrolled resource exploitation.
21. In addition, approximately one quarter of the areas under AUSCEM have lapsed due to lack of support and
technical assistance for legal proceedings and management activities, leaving these mangrove areas without
effective protection. Furthermore, despite most mangrove residents, being highly dependent on natural
resources and ecosystems, they have limited awareness of climate change. Training, participatory planning
and strengthening of livelihoods alternatives in mangrove areas can therefore contribute to enhancing climate
change resilience.

22. The Socio Manglar program (meaning “Mangrove Partner”) builds on AUSCEMs and was established by
Ministerial Decree No. 198 on July 9, 2014, by the Ministry of the Environment, as part of the Socio Bosque
program (SBP) of conservation incentives, which has been transformative within Ecuador as a mechanism for
engaging local communities in the stewardship of native forests23. The Socio Manglar program is designed to
provide direct economic payments to beneficiary AUSCEM groups to support the conservation of mangroves
and supplement communities’ income. The objective of the Socio Manglar program is to complement and
consolidate the results achieved through the AUSCEMs granted to communities and ancestral groups of users,
to guarantee the conservation of mangroves while improving the living conditions of the population dependent
on fisheries resources. (Note: Socio Manglar payments are only used in the protection of mangroves, it is not
financial support for shrimp cultivation.)
23. In 2020, Socio Manglar payments by the MAATE to AUSCEMs totaled US$379,563. Current Socio Manglar
payments come from MAATE’s core budget and contributions from the REDD Early Movers program supported
by the German government (see Annex 2, Appendix 12) Currently only 33,467 ha of mangroves under 25
AUSCEMs receive Socio Manglar incentive payment agreements, leaving significant scope for expansion within
.
remaining active AUSCEMs (26,750 ha) as well as in potential new AUSCEM areas.

sustainable community enterprises have been identified by stakeholders as interventions that would contribute
to further reducing deforestation and strengthening of mangrove stewardship. The Socio Manglar subaccount,

a subaccount of the existing Socio Bosque Fund, has been established in preparation for this project and as a
mechanism for developing the long-term financial sustainability of the Socio Manglar Program. The Socio
Manglar subaccount is managed as part of the Socio Bosque Fund with the intent that it operates as an
endowment, with the interest and investment income from its investments used to pay for incentives to
AUSCEMs. Component 2 of the project includes a request for $4 million from GCF to help capitalize the Socio
Manglar subaccount. The GCF funds will be kept separately from the other Socio Manglar subaccount funds in
an interest-bearing account, while other funds will be kept in an investment account and invested according to
the investment strategy of the fund manager, Fondo de Inversion Ambiental Sostenible (FIAS), which is
Ecuador’s Environmental and Sustainable Investing Fund. Additional contributions will be sought for the Socio
Manglar subaccount from private sector companies operating in Ecuador as part of project activities in
Component 2.
25. While there have not yet been evaluations of the Socio Manglar program, due to its recent establishment,
several evaluations of the SBP highlight that deforestation rates are lower on land included within the SBP and
that communities gain important monetary and non-monetary benefits (Arriaga et al. 2018, Perafán and Pabón,
2019). The most important impacts identified by the evaluations were increased land and rights-use, security,
economic development, and organizational support. Communities also reported non-monetary benefits due to
collective decision making and social cohesion from working together to achieve a common goal. However, the
evaluations noted the need to strengthen financial sustainability of the program and the need to address
conflicts that can arise if some communities receive incentive benefits while others do not (Etchart et al., 2020).
In the case of mangrove areas, there is a stronger possibility to monetize the traditional economies because
the economic value of non-timber products (i.e., from fisheries) is much higher than for terrestrial forests.

GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0 | PAGE 13
B
26. This project will scale and expand the results and lessons learned from previous initiatives, including
complementarity with GEF projects. As included in table 25 of Annex 2, five GEF projects have contributed to
the creation of capacities and field experience on marine protected areas (MPA), enforcing local associations,
local planning and financial sustainability. Conservation International – Ecuador (CI-E) is also currently
implementing a project with the GMA that seeks to create networks among organizations working with mangrove
ecosystems and support AUSCEMs in the management of fisheries.
National Protected Areas
27. Forty-one percent of all mangroves of mainland Ecuador lie within the boundaries of nine PAs, making these
an important strategy for mangrove management and conservation. These nine PAs cover three different
categories of PAs: i) Ecological Reserves (Reserva Ecológica – RE); ii) Flora and Fauna Production Reserves
(Reserva de Producción de Flora y Fauna – RPF); and iii) Wildlife Refuges (Reserva de Vida Silvestre – RVS).
28. Four PAs, prioritized for inclusion in the project contain 94.6% of mangroves in PAs:
● Reserva Ecológica Manglares Cayapas Mataje (includes 20,012 ha of mangrove)
● Reserva Ecológica Manglares Churute (includes 28,467 ha of mangrove)
● Reserva de Producción de Flora y Fauna Manglares El Salado (includes 11,659 ha of mangrove)
● Reserva de Vida Silvestre Manglares Estuario del Rio Muisne (includes 1,295 ha of mangrove)
Mangrove reforestation and restoration
29. Ecuador has established and executed successful mangrove reforestation efforts. Techniques for mangrove
reforestation are well-proven in Ecuador, and from other sites around the world (see Annex 2, Feasibility
Study). An effective policy intervention that led to an increase in mangrove cover was Executive Decree 1391
in 2008, which required many shrimp farms that had been illegally established in public coastal areas, to restore
significant areas of mangrove on or adjacent to their farms, and allowed a three-year grace period for
registration and starting reforestation activities, before legalization of the shrimp farms. This resulted in a surge
in mangrove restoration (totaling 3,110 ha) immediately following its issue. Unfortunately, since the policy
ended, no new areas are being registered and reforested under this mechanism. Sustaining or increasing the
rate of mangrove recovery now depends on other regulatory instruments and on voluntary actions, which are
the principal focus of this project.
30. Conservation International (CI) has identified over 10,000 ha of land in the four estuaries appropriate for
potential reforestation based on criteria including size, form, connection with remaining mangroves, and
vulnerability of populations. Of these areas, 4,850 ha will be restored as part of the GCF project (see Annex 2
for further details of the prioritization and restoration methods) to provide long-term flood protection benefits,
carbon sequestration and ecosystem service co-benefits in the form of improved fisheries for vulnerable people.
B.1.4 Governance
Planning and zoning
31. Local governments are known as Decentralized Autonomous Governments (GADs24) in Ecuador and include
subnational entities at the provincial, municipal (or cantonal) and parish levels with a degree of administrative
and regulatory autonomy. GADs need to play an important role in climate governance and, in coordination with
the national government, collaborate on integrated coastal management. A fundamental instrument for planning
and investment of the GADs are Development and Land Use Plans (Planes de Desarrollo y Ordenamiento
Territorial - PDOT). PDOTs are a key tool to ensure that decision-making processes are based on information
collected at the territorial level, and the implementation of measures that reduce the impact of climate threats
on planned programs or projects is considered. PDOTs also provide the opportunity to ensure a strong marine
and coastal spatial planning approach in the mangrove zone of the coastal region of Ecuador. These PDOTs
can be complemented, under Ecuadorian law (Organic Code for the Environment – CODA), by Management
Plans for Beaches and the Coastal Strip addressing the specific needs and characteristics within and across
multiple GADs. Three municipalities have so far established such plans. Mangrove areas and climate change
vulnerabilities are generally not currently adequately addressed in PDOTs.
Enforcement
32. Legal enforcement has faced a series of setbacks when trying to enforce mangrove conservation measures
with shrimp farm owners who expanded shrimp farms without prior authorization from the National
24 Gobiernos Autónomos Descentralizados

GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0 | PAGE 14
B
Environmental Authority. Although regulatory frameworks are in place, enforcement is inadequate for protection
and restoration of mangroves by public, private and community-based actors.
33. Operational Control and Surveillance Units (UOCVs – Unidades Operativas de Control y Vigilancia) were piloted
for detection and law enforcement of illegal activities affecting mangroves as part of the Program for the
Management of Coastal Resources project. As a joint effort between the Navy, MAATE, Vice-ministry of
Aquaculture and Fisheries (VMAP), NGOs and the National Chamber of Aquaculture (NCA), the UOCVs’ main
goal was to avoid and control the clear-cutting of mangrove forest areas through the cooperation of public,
private and NGO entities involved in this environmental issue.
34. The application of sanctions for environmental crimes under the Organic Code for Criminal Processes (Código
Orgánico Procesal Penal) is also limited by gaps in knowledge about regulations, including administrative
procedures and fines. When environmental crimes and sanctions are taken up by the courts, there is frequently
a lack of knowledge on the part of prosecutors and judges regarding marine and coastal laws and regulations,
and a lack of coordination and collaboration between prosecutors and the MAATE.
B.1.5 Private sector initiatives: Shrimp aquaculture
35. Shrimp farming has historically been the single biggest driver of mangrove deforestation in Ecuador and
continues to account for around half of anthropogenic mangrove loss. In 2020, there were 3,933 registered
shrimp farms in Ecuador operating on 216,000 ha (Seafood Watch, 2021). Effectively engaging with the shrimp
aquaculture sector, through better enforcement and through voluntary action, is an important element of
effective mangrove conservation and restoration.
36. Shrimp aquaculture in Ecuador is recognized for using low stocking density production (extensive production)
to decrease disease risks, although the sector remains GHG intensive. The low stocking density strategy, widely
adopted after disease outbreaks in the 1990s severely impacted national production but does produce a high-
quality product with lower environmental impacts. Overall growth of the sector has increased from about 50,000
Mt in 2000 to 700,000 Mt in 2020 (Chase, 2021). Shrimp production intensity averages 1.92 t/ha/y in Ecuador,
which is significantly lower than intensive production seen in Thailand (18.2 t/ha/y) and below production
intensity averages of China, India, and Indonesia (4.5 t/ha/y, 3.6 t/ha/y, and 3.3 t/ha/y, respectively) (CEA
Consulting, 2018).
Opportunities for engagement with the private sector
37. The shrimp aquaculture industry has had limited engagement with mangrove conservation, despite the role of
mangroves in protecting their vulnerable coastal infrastructure, regulatory requirements for reforestation, and
increasing market demand for sustainable production. Increasing private-sector participation is constrained
mainly by lack of information about the benefits of mangroves, limited technical capacity to identify and adopt
practices that restore or conserve mangroves, and in the case of small shrimp farmers, limited access to
affordable finance for all aspects of their operations. However, previous efforts by aquaculture companies to
restore mangroves following Decree 1391 were generally successful and survival rates of mangrove seedlings
were high despite localized problems (Pesante & Rosero, 2020). These previous experiences of mangrove
restoration in Ecuador mean there is capacity to expand restoration efforts.
Beyond the need for legal compliance, market opportunities and barriers are also driving a shift within Ecuador’s
shrimp aquaculture sector. Voluntary, market-facing certification and labeling schemes are drawing increasing
38. attention as a way to differentiate Ecuadorian shrimp as sustainable, healthy and good for the environment.
Depending on the retailer, certification is seen as: i)a requirement for sourcing (without additional pricing), or
ii) a value-added product that retailers and consumers concerned with environmental sustainability and social
responsibility are willing to pay more for on a per k ilogram basis. While there has been growing interest in
sustainable practices in the shrimp aquaculture sector, less than 1% of all shrimp farms in Ecuador currently
operate under certification schemes that align with climate resilience and emissions reductions. Several
complementary initiatives are underway that present an opportunity for scaling and improving practices,
including Aquaculture Stewardship Council (ASC) certification and the Sustainable Shrimp Partnership (SSP).
39. ASC provides a certification standard to minimize unintended social and environmental impacts of aquaculture
production around the world. ASC certified shrimp farms must minimize impacts on their neighboring ecosystem
in several ways, such as the partial restoration of lost mangrove forest, the development and implementation of
a biodiversity-focused environmental impact assessment (B-EIA) and ensuring farms are not sited in critical
habitats. Furthermore, since shrimp farming often occurs along coastal areas, a permanent coastal barrier must
be in place between the farm and the coastline. Ecuador has one of the largest areas of shrimp farms certified
under ASC; approximately 15% of production is certified, with a total volume (including shrimp and tilapia) of

=== PAGE 22 ===
GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0 | PAGE 15
B
75,000 Mt sold in 2020. Currently, 15 companies are participating in ASC with 28 certified farms (49 sites) in
Ecuador. Products from ASC-certified farms and chains of custody can display the ASC logo to improve market
access and the perceived value to consumers of their products.
40. The NCA has also been working on the development and implementation of the national SPP initiative to create
a brand to differentiate the Ecuadorian product in the global seafood market. The SSP is an industry led initiative
that builds on the ASC criteria adding additional criteria, including a neutral impact on water25.. With
approximately 3,000 shrimp farms in Ecuador, there is significant scope for growth for both the ASC and SSP,
leveraging market access and differentiation to achieve environmental outcomes, including carbon neutrality
and mangrove restoration and protection; with their requirements to eliminate mangrove deforestation, the ASC
and SSP can contribute directly to reducing emissions from mangrove conversion if widely adopted (see Annex
2 Feasibility Study for further details).
41. CI’s Climate-Smart Shrimp (CSS) initiative provides a model for climate change adaptation within the sector.
CSS is complementary to the ASC and SSP standards and is a novel set of practices that prevent deforestation
and incentivize mangrove restoration while also increasing the amount of shrimp a farm can produce. CSS
involves working with shrimp pond farmers to sustainably intensify shrimp production, construct treatment
wetlands, and restore coastal ecosystems, while securing buyers for the ‘CSS’ produced (Figure 4). For small
and medium farmers, the primary incentives to participate are access to capital and technical assistance, while
for larger farmers, who often already have access to capital and expertise, the incentive is connections to
sustainability-oriented buyers in North American and European markets who preferentially source responsibly
produced shrimp. The CSS approach can realize climate change resilience and adaptation benefits while also
increasing production and profitability to meet development goals.
Establishing the shrimp industry as a partner in mangrove restoration will increase funding for restoration and
expand the number and diversity of stakeholders conserving, protecting, and rebuilding natural infrastructure at
42. scale, and eliminate pressures for deforestation. The project includes activities with Ecuadorian shrimp
producers to expand the use of ASC/SPP certification, directly restore mangroves and generate additional
financial contributions for the Socio Manglar Program that will support community-based mangrove
conservation and management beyond the implementation period of the project.
Figure 4. A visual comparison of the “gray” alternative to intensification (middle) versus applying a green-
gray climate adaptation approach to sustainably intensify shrimp production using the CSS model (right).
Conditions for private sector engagement
43. To promote CSS or any type of certification, a key element is ensuring that adequate and accessible financing
exists to address the needs of shrimp farmers. An important barrier to access finance is the lack of technical
25 https://www.sustainableshrimppartnership.org/

=== PAGE 23 ===
GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0 | PAGE 16
B
knowledge within financial institutions about the industry and its sustainability pathways. Establishing
partnerships that allow access to information, training and implementation of demonstration cases facilitates
the learning curve so that the shrimp sector becomes more attractive and less risky for financial institutions.
This is the approach of the eco.business Fund26, a US$390 million fund that provides financial services to
financial intermediaries in the Latin America region, in support of green economy transformation. The fund has
ambitious targets for the aquaculture sector in Ecuador; and helps financial institutions develop tools, skills and
demonstration cases for key investments. Alliances with key stakeholders, including CI and its model of CSS,
ASC and SPP, are critical to strengthening the content and scientific approach and influencing the target
audience for those trainings.
44. Besides lack of knowledge, there are other important barriers to access finance, such as early-stage companies
with unstable cash flows or lack of collateral to access traditional funding sources like the banking system.
Investment funds fill some of these gaps with a diversity of instruments such as debt and equity that can adapt
to specific needs and business stages. To further fill this gap, CI is developing a fund with a specific focus on
supporting adoption of the CSS approach. The Climate Smart Shrimp Fund (CSSF)27 will provide loan
packages, supported by a technical assistance facility, that will enable shrimp farmers to transition to more
sustainable and efficient production systems while simultaneously restoring mangrove ecosystems. The initial
objective is to create a US$100 million fund28 with a focus on supporting CSS in Indonesia, Ecuador and the
Philippines. Pilot testing of the CSSF is currently underway with a company in Ecuador.
Other opportunities to work with the private sector
45. In addition to Ecuador’s shrimp sector, there may be opportunities to work with international companies whose
interest in investing in mangrove conservation and restoration is driven by their own Environmental, Social,
Governance strategies. CI will seek out such opportunities to build long term support for community-based
conservation and forest restoration (see project activity 2.2.1).
B 1.6 The impacts of coastal flooding events in the project area
46. Based on review of local data sets and a series of models that were applied to Ecuador´s coast (see Appendix
6 of Annex 2, Feasibility Study), the project will reduce flood risk for at least 89,600 people:
• In coastal mangrove areas, approximately 198,000 people are exposed annually to high flood risks from
either coastal or riparian flooding (SNGR, 2018). Of those, approximately 22,400 are estimated to have
an annual risk of flooding from coastal storm surges even with current mangrove cover (based on the
methodology of Menéndez et al., 2020; see below and Annex 25 for more details).
• Without the protective functions of the current mangrove cover, an additional 86,200 people would have
an annual risk of flooding29 due to coastal storm surge events (based on the methodology of Menéndez
2020; see Annex 25 for more details of calculations). These 86,200 people will benefit from the project
since it will strengthen the protection of existing mangroves and therefore ensure that the flood protection
continues.
• By ensuring that current mangrove areas are conserved, deforestation due to shrimp farming is reduced
by 50% and mangrove coverage is expanded by 4,850 ha through restoration, the project will benefit an
estimated 3,40030 of the 22,400 people currently exposed to annual flood risk (see Annex 25). The figure
of 3,400 is based on a calculation of the average number of people that derive flood protection benefit
from one hectare of mangroves in Ecuador. The project will therefore provide improved flood protection
to 89,600 people (assumed to be 50% female, 50% male).
Demography of the population in the project area (See Annex 2, Feasibility Study for further detail on the
demographics in the project area and the Gender Assessment and Action Plan (Annex 8) for specific gendered
uses and management of mangroves.)
47. Three million, four hundred thousand people live in the municipalities of the estuaries targeted by the project.
The sex ratio is 50.9% males; 49.1% females. The population is 83% urban, due to the inclusion of some urban
26 https://www.ecobusiness.fund/en/
27 https://www.climatefinancelab.org/idea s/climate-smart-shrimp-fund/
28 The CSS Fund is being developed as a blended finance vehicle, with approximately 30% concessional finance and 70% commercial
finance. The fund's long-term goal is to make the case to local financial institutions that they can/should make CSS loans. The purpose of
the CSS Fund is to create a track record of successful CSS loans (as defined by loan repayment at commercial rates, farm profitability, and
mangrove restoration) that show the attractiveness of making CSS loans to commercial investors (including local financial institutions).

B
areas of Guayaquil and smaller towns in the project area. Poverty levels average 72%. Unstructured, rapid
urbanization in Ecuador’s coastal towns and cities have pushed poor urban dwellers into low-lying areas around
waterways that are the most prone to flooding, leaving them highly vulnerable to climate change. Across all
project sites, women and men depend on the natural resources they obtain from the mangroves, including fish
and shellfish such as crabs and mollusks (especially black clam). In exceptional circumstances, such as during
the closed fishing season, men find they need to earn an income outside the home. Further information on
specific gendered uses and management of mangroves is detailed in the Gender Assessment and Action
Plan (Annex 8).
The impacts of flooding events on local communities and key economic sectors
48. The hazard level of coastal flooding in all areas of the project is classified as the highest hazard level, by the
Global Facility for Disaster Reduction and Recovery (GFDRR), meaning that potentially damaging waves are
expected to flood the coast at least once in the next ten years31. The southern coastal region of Ecuador (the
region of the two southern estuaries included in the project) has been identified as the location with the highest
coastal risk in the Latin America and Caribbean region due to a combination of coastal hazards, geographic
exposure, and socio-economic vulnerability (Calil et al., 2017).
49. Flooding events have become more frequent over the last 30 years; and are particularly acute during El Niño
events in which rainfall can rise as high as 3,500 mm per year. The Borbor-Cordova et al. (2020) study
recommends that coastal cities in Ecuador must recognize that they are part of larger scale ecosystems and
that they need to integrate coastal ecosystem services from mangroves and other wetlands into urban and
landscape design.
50. In a global assessment, Ecuador’s shrimp production has been highlighted as having the highest vulnerability
to climate change of the world´s brackish aquaculture producers, based on exposure (temperature, water
balance, population density, precipitation change, flood risk, drought risk and cyclone risk), adaptive capacity
and sensitivity (Handisyde et al., 2017). Eighty-two percent of shrimp farms along Ecuador’s coast are sited in
areas classified as high flood risk under current conditions (MAP, 2018; SNGR, 2018), risks which will increase
dramatically with increased sea level and intensified precipitation projected due to climate change. During the
1997-98 El Niño event, flooding, disruption of supply chains and damage to transport infrastructure affected the
shrimp aquaculture sector; around 4,500 ha of ponds were severely damaged (CAF, 2000). Heavy rains and
flooding can also affect the harvest cycle, causing shrimp to molt early, resulting in up to a 20% drop in yields
per pond (Sackton, 2016), while severe weather events can cause partial or total loss of harvest. These
damages have direct consequences in the form of loss of income. The shrimp aquaculture sector should
therefore have an incentive to support measures and adopt practices that help reduce current and future flood
risk.

B 1.7 Importance of mangroves in providing coastal protection benefits against flooding and climate
change
51. While mangroves provide a diversity of ecosystem services, their value for coastal protection and the reduction
of flood risks is particularly well documented and relevant for Ecuador’s coast. The use of "green infrastructure"
in the form of natural ecosystems to reduce the effects of climate change hazards, for example to dissipate
wave energy and reduce the intensity of storm surges, has been well documented (see additional detail in
Annex 2). For example, mangrove species have dense roots to reduce wave energy and height (Figure 5) such
that wave height is decreased as the waves travel through the mangroves. Several studies highlight the
importance of re-establishing wide and intact tracts of mangrove to maximize their coastal flood protection
benefit. Storm surges can raise water levels on the coast for long periods (hours or days), compared to waves
that come from normal winds or tides and that last for shorter times (seconds or minutes). This further highlights
the importance of having wide tracts of mangroves to reduce storm surges.
Figure 5. Key factors contributing to wave attenuation. Source: McIvor et al., 2012b
Modelling the flood protection benefits of mangroves
52. To model the flood protection benefits of mangroves we applied the ‘avoided damage’ valuation approach
described by Menéndez et al. (2020), which uses the cost of damages prevented by mangroves to estimate
their value. (see Appendix 6 of Annex 2). The ‘avoided damage’ analysis using the Menéndez model does not
evaluate future climate scenarios, nor does it directly estimate benefits of adding mangroves, only the damage
caused if existing mangroves are removed. However, it does provide per hectare estimates of the value of
mangroves in terms of annual expected risk of flooding and reduced damage, which has been used in the
preparation of this project to estimate the benefits of increasing mangrove cover.

Figure 6. Projected flooding in Ecuador with (in blue) and without (in red) mangroves for a 1 in 25-year
storm event based on the methodology of Menéndez et al. (2020).
53. The Menéndez model analysis concludes that based on current conditions, 22,400 people have an annual
expected risk of coastal flooding caused by storm surge (16,900 in urban areas and 5,500 in rural areas). Under
these current climate conditions, but without the protections that mangroves provide, approximately 86,200
more people would be at annual risk of coastal flooding in the project area. The analysis also shows that the
mangroves prevent approximately US$250 million of property loss from flooding annually. Based upon
mangrove coverage in Ecuador from 2018 (approximately 156,633 ha), every hectare of mangrove prevents
approximately US$1,600 of property loss from coastal flooding every year in Ecuador. Property loss in this case
includes both industrial and residential stock where damage and property loss values increase with increasing
flood depth. Based on these figures, approximately one person has reduced annual expected flood risk for each
two hectares of mangrove in Ecuador32. As noted above, currently urban areas account for most of the people
at risk and therefore concentrating mangrove protection and restoration efforts between the sea and urban
areas will have the most impact. This is particularly the case in the Gulf of Guayaquil, where most urban
inhabitants reside.
32 Or, more precisely 1 hectare of mangrove provides protection for 0.58 people on average.

=== PAGE 27 ===
GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0 | PAGE 20
B
54. In preparation of the project, an alternative modelling approach has also been used (using the Aqueduct Flood
model, which does include future climate change related flooding risk) and is presented in Annex 2, Feasibility
Study. This alternative model concurs with the Menéndez et al. (2020) in projecting that most people and
property impacted by annual coastal floods in Ecuador live in Guayas. The important conclusion from this model
is that with climate change, the relative value of mangroves to reduce coastal flooding increases. The two
modelling approaches used also converge on a similar number of people being currently affected by coastal
flooding during storm surges. Neither of the models includes potential changes in population due to migration
patterns, which would include an additional large degree of uncertainty in the estimates for the future population
at risk of flooding. We have therefore assumed for the purposes of this project, that the figure of 89,600 people
with reduced annual expected risk from coastal surge flooding is a conservative estimate of the number of
people benefitting from the additional coastal flood protection that the project will achieve by reducing forest
loss and increasing mangrove cover through restoration.
Summary of Climate Ch ange H azards and Ri sk/Impacts
Hazard: 1. Increase in flooding
Coastal Ecuador is already exposed to frequent flooding from both riparian floods due to high rainfall
and coastal surges associated with storms. The frequency of coastal surges will increase as sea
level rise increases (see Hazard 2 below) and extreme El Niño events (see Hazard 3 below)
exacerbate the impacts of storms.
Risk/ In coastal mangrove areas, approximately 198,000 people are currently exposed annually to high
Impact33: flood risks from either coastal or riparian flooding (SNGR, 2018).
By ensuring that current mangrove areas are conserved, deforestation due to human pressures
(mostly shrimp farming) is reduced by 50% and restoration expanding mangrove coverage by 4,850
ha, the project will benefit at least 89,600 by protecting them from flood risk from coastal surges
(see Annex 25).
Hallegatte et al. (2013) estimated the relative risk of vulnerability to flooding (economic average
annual losses/city’s GDP) in 136 coastal port cities, by assessing city-level flood risk and a database
of urban coastal protection in 2005 and showed that the city of Guayaquil (which is protected by the
mangroves included in the project) is ranked number four globally for flood risk. When future socio-
economic changes are included, as well as a modest sea level rise scenario (20 cm increase), the
assumption of adaptation measures by 2050, the increase in the economic average losses
increases by 13%, putting the city of Guayaquil within the top 14 of flooding-related economic losses
expected between 2005 and 2050 (Hallegatte et al., 2013)
Ecuador’s shrimp production has been highlighted as having the highest vulnerability to climate
change of the world´s brackish aquaculture producers in a global assessment, based on exposure
(temperature, water balance, population density, precipitation change, flood risk, drought risk and
cyclone risk), adaptive capacity and sensitivity (Handisyde et al., 2017). Eighty-two percent of
shrimp farms along Ecuador’s coast are sited in areas classified as high flood risk under current
conditions (Ministerio de Acuacultura y Pesca, 2018; Secretaría Nacional de Gestión de Riesgos,
2018), risks which will increase with increased sea level and intensified precipitation projected due
to climate change. During the 1997-98 El Niño, flooding, disruption of supply chains and damage to
transport infrastructure affected the shrimp aquaculture sector and around 4,500 ha of ponds were
severely damaged34 (CAF, 2000). Heavy rains and flooding can also affect the harvest cycle,
causing shrimp to molt early, resulting in up to a 20% drop in yields per pond (Sackton, 2016). These
damages have direct consequences for the more than 60,000 people employed (approximately 0.45
jobs/ha of shrimp farm) in shrimp aquaculture farming and processing (Yahira Piedrahita, 2018) 35.
33 Assess the impact, that is the effects on natural and human systems of extreme weather and climate change events. Impacts generally
refer to effects on lives, livelihoods, healt h, ecosystems, economies, societies, cultures, services, and infrastructure due to the interaction of
climate changes or hazardous climate change events occurring within a specific time period and the vulnerability of an exposed society or
system.
34 Clark Labs data estimates the presence of 1,571,298 km2 of aquaculture ponds in Ecuador in 1999 (source:
http://www.aquaculture.earth/coastal/index.html)
35 Other estimates for the shrimp industry reference 100,000 - 220,000 directly or indirectly employed.

--- Table 1 on Page 27 ---
54. In preparation of the project, an alternative modelling approach has also been used (using the Aqueduct Flood
model, which does include future climate change related flooding risk) and is presented in Annex 2, Feasibility
Study. This alternative model concurs with the Menéndez et al. (2020) in projecting that most people and
property impacted by annual coastal floods in Ecuador live in Guayas. The important conclusion from this model
is that with climate change, the relative value of mangroves to reduce coastal flooding increases. The two
modelling approaches used also converge on a similar number of people being currently affected by coastal
flooding during storm surges. Neither of the models includes potential changes in population due to migration
patterns, which would include an additional large degree of uncertainty in the estimates for the future population
at risk of flooding. We have therefore assumed for the purposes of this project, that the figure of 89,600 people
with reduced annual expected risk from coastal surge flooding is a conservative estimate of the number of
people benefitting from the additional coastal flood protection that the project will achieve by reducing forest
loss and increasing mangrove cover through restoration.
Summary of Climate Ch ange H azards and Ri sk/Impacts
Hazard: 1. Increase in flooding
Coastal Ecuador is already exposed to frequent flooding from both riparian floods due to high rainfall
and coastal surges associated with storms. The frequency of coastal surges will increase as sea
level rise increases (see Hazard 2 below) and extreme El Niño events (see Hazard 3 below)
exacerbate the impacts of storms.

Direct consequences include loss of income from lower production or when severe weather events
cause partial or total loss of harvest.
See section 2.2.3 “Communities at H ighest Risk from Flooding” in Annex 2: Feasibility Study for
more details.
Hazard: 2. Sea Level rise
Sea Level Rise (SLR) will have significant direct impacts on Ecuador’s coast and its increase is a
climate-change driven phenomenon. By 2100, mean sea level is projected to rise an additional 49-
64 cm on Ecuador's coast36 depending on the Shared Socioeconomic Pathways (SSP) scenario
(see Table 6; Annex 2: Feasibility Study). Measured mean sea level increased at an average
annual rate of approximately 0.8 cm (1990-2009) based on the tide gauge of La Libertad (Alavera
& Nath, 2013), implying that these projections are conservative (Figure 7; Annex 2: Feasibility
study).
An increase in mean sea level will also be compounded by increased frequency of extreme sea
level (ESL) events (storm surge and tidal flooding), which will become common by the end of the
century and occur annually mid-century in low-lying coastal areas (IPCC, 2019).
Risk/ Increases in SLR and ESL are expected to increase the risks of coastal flooding and consequently
Impact: impact rural and urban populations, and aquaculture in coastal areas, especially when compounded
by periodic El Niño events. Celemin (2018), in an analysis of Ecuador´s northern coastal province
of Esmeraldas, posited extensive damage related to a 5 m increase in mean sea level, though this
extreme scenario is outside the range of most other projections.
Hazard: 3. Extreme El Niño Events
El Niño events have already led to observed ocean warming and dramatic rises in sea level due to
seawater expansion (Cai et al., 2014; Wang et al., 2019). During the 1997-98 season, sea surface
temperatures off the coast of Ecuador increased by 5°C above normal, and sea level increased up
to 42 cm (CAF, 2000). In the equatorial Pacific region, sea levels can deviate from the global mean
sea level by as much as 40 cm because of El Nino (Walsh et al., 2012).
ENSO is a natural climatic driver, and it is very likely that rainfall variability related to ENSO will be
amplified by the second half of the 21st century under the SSP 2-4.5, SSP 3-7.0 and SSP 5-8.5
scenarios (IPCC, 2021). There are indications that extreme El Niño events may increase in
frequency due to climate change (McPhaden et al., 2020). Future El Niño events are likely to
become more frequent and more intense (Magrin et al., 2007; Wang et al., 2019), but even an El
Niño of similar magnitude to historical events, will affect larger areas and more people as its flooding
impacts are compounded by sea level rise.
Risk/ Historical El Niño events provide a reference point for the scale of future climate-related damages
Impact: for Ecuador’s coast. The Dartmouth Flood Observatory registered 22 major flooding events since
1987, with over 506,000 people displaced (DFO, 2020). Flooding events were particularly severe
during El Niño events of 1972/73, 1982/83, 1997/98, as well as in 2008 and 2012, with 184,000-
222,000 ha flooded (Ocles Padilla, 2018). Flooding events registered since 1965 on the coast of
Ecuador have affected over 1,822,704 people, who required immediate assistance during those
events, with 68% of those occurring during El Nino years. During those flooding events, 97,031
people became homeless. Flooding events recorded since 1965 have killed 836 people, with 622
of those occurring during El Nino years (Em-DAT, 2021). Those flooding events led to over US$ 1.5
billion in damages and economic losses, of which 96% occurred during years when El Niño events
occurred (EM-DAT, 2021). Furthermore, one quarter of the total coastal population was exposed to
increased health risks in the form of infectious diseases such as malaria, diarrhea and cholera,
which are associated with floods and damages to sanitary infrastructure caused by the 1997/98 El
Niño event (Vos et al., 1999).
36 https://sealevel.nasa.gov/ipcc-ar6-sea-level-projection-tool The SLR range cited is based on projections for scenario SSP2-4.5 (lower
estimate) and SSP3-7.0 (higher estimate ).

B.2 (a). Theory of change narrative and diagram (max. 1500 words, approximately 3 pages plus diagram)
Current Conditions and Trends
55. Current trends and drivers of emissions and vulnerability are described in greater detail in section B1 and Annex
2, but key points for the Theory of Change are summarized here for clarity.
56. Sea level rise (~12 cm by 2040 and 49-64 cm by 2100)37, increased impacts of El Niño events, and augmented
intensity and variability of precipitation due to climate change are projected to increase flood frequency, intensity
and damages, thereby threatening coastal infrastructure, settlements and economic activities.
57. Flood impacts are concentrated in recurring El Niño events and other extreme sea level events. SLR will magnify
El Niño damages.
58. In addition to maintaining biodiversity and many ecosystem services, mangroves play a dual role, both in
reducing climate change vulnerability of coastal communities to flooding events, and in increasing carbon
storage. Although mangrove areas are slowly recovering and increasing (+0.35% per year)38, an average of
990 ha of primary mangrove forests are still lost each year. Areas under community management and PAs are
gaining mangrove coverage while unprotected areas continue to have net loss of mangrove.
59. Nearly 96% of all mangrove areas are concentrated in a subset of eight municipalities providing important flood
protection for people and assets. With measures to protect and restore mangroves, the current flood protection
benefits will be secured for the future and 89,600 people are expected to benefit annually by the end of the
project (see Annex 25). The project is also expected to provide more resilient livelihoods for 41,500 climate-
vulnerable direct beneficiaries. CI conservatively assumes that all these 41,500 people will be included within
the 89,600 direct beneficiaries and therefore, to avoid double counting, estimates the overall number of direct
beneficiaries of the project to be 89,600.
60. Shrimp aquaculture, which accounted for half of mangrove loss this past decade, also accounted for US$6.2
billion in exports in 2022 (MercoPress, 2023). Increasing recognition of the threats that climate change poses
to the sector and growing demand for sustainably produced shrimp have created an opportunity for shrimp
aquaculture operations to play a pivotal role in protecting and restoring mangroves. Other private sector actors,
including investors and companies seeking to become ‘carbon-neutral’, create additional opportunities for
private sector finance for mangroves.
37 https://sealevel.nasa.gov/ipcc-ar6-sea-level-projection-tool with 2100 range calculated based on the SSP2-4.5 and SSP3-7.0 scenarios.
38 The recovery is in part due to short-live d regulatory measures adopted in 2008 that no longer apply.

B.2 (a). Theory of change narrative and diagram (max. 1500 words, approximately 3 pages plus diagram)
Current Conditions and Trends
55. Current trends and drivers of emissions and vulnerability are described in greater detail in section B1 and Annex
2, but key points for the Theory of Change are summarized here for clarity.

intensity and variability of precipitation due to climate change are projected to increase flood frequency, intensity
