GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0 | PAGE 43
B
147. CI will work with local governments to enforce their capacity to manage their territories with a clear perspective
of integrated coastal zone management and marine spatial planning that are critically important to realizing
flood protection benefits from mangroves as well as other ecosystem services from coastal ecosystems. CI will
work with nine local governments/administrations to integrate natural climate change adaptation measures into
their planning, including mangrove conservation and restoration. For this, CI will work following PDOT´s
guidelines from the National Planning Secretariat that includes adaptation and mitigation actions . The local
governments have been prioritized based on the extent of their mangrove cover. To support this w49ork, CI will
provide a subgrant in Year 2 to a local organization (university or NGO to be competitively selected) to generate
climate risk information to inform local planning.
148. CI will work with five parish administrations (Parroquias) to develop climate change risk-management plans that
value impacts and involvement of men and women. CI will provide technical support to these local governments
for planning that will help them increase the economic, social, health and cultural resilience of individuals and
communities. The risk-management plans will include activities for monitoring and evaluation of the adaptive
capacity of men and women considering their access and control of resources, participation and roles within
organizational processes.
149. CI will work with two municipal governments (Guayaquil and Machala), responsible for land-use planning and
zoning through their PDOTs. CI will provide technical input and facilitation of participatory planning processes
to integrate strategies for climate-change adaptation, including strategies for conservation and restoration of
mangroves.
150. CI will support two provincial governments (Guayas and El Oro) with technical support to integrate climate
change considerations into the provincial planning and to support budget planning to promote additional climate
change investment. These activities will support management of priority conservation areas, local fishermen,
strengthen capacities for the preparation of management plans for the coastal areas, and develop land use and
management plans as well as integrated coastal management plans. The project will also support the integration
into the provincial plans of actions to create the enabling conditions necessary for the long-term conservation
and restoration of mangroves.
151. CI will enter into service agreements with consultants beginning in Year 2 to provide territorial planning support
to local governments for these activities. The CI Local Governance & Integrated Coastal Management Specialist
will provide support to these government entities across the four estuaries of the project.
152. CI will provide ongoing support, monitoring and public communication for the implementation of plans,
corresponding public investments and municipal ordinances and other legal instruments determined by
PDOTs.
Activity 3.2.2 Strengthen regulatory framework and law enforcement by agencies and institutions responsible for
control of mangroves, with a focus on human rights.
153. CI staff and consultants engaged by CI will conduct an analysis of multi-sectoral legal framework for mangroves
and climate change and the creation of multi-sectoral working groups (“mesas técnicas”) to generate proposals
for regulatory changes in Environment, Vice-ministry of Aquaculture and Fisheries (VMAP), Navy, Ports, and
GADs. Examples of such changes may include updates to the regulations regarding illegal shrimp farms not
covered by Executive Decree 1391, which have not been expropriated or approved, procedures for the
restoration of mangroves, and regulation of areas that were formerly mangroves.
154. CI will also support the discussion of legal reforms and new regulations in the context of the Roundtable for
Sustainable Shrimp, to be convened by the MAATE and VMAP. CI will provide staff time and cover workshop
costs to implement this roundtable.
155. In Years 2 to 5, CI’s Legal Specialist and Local Governance & Integrated Coastal Management Specialist will
work with partners to re-establish the strategy of the UOCVs for law enforcement and sanctions for illegal
activities affecting mangroves. A joint effort between the Oceanographic Institute of the Navy (INOCAR),
MAATE, VMAP, NGOs and the NCA, the UOCVs’ main goal is to avoid and control the clear-cutting of mangrove
forest areas through the cooperation of public, private and NGO entities. The implementation of this initiative

B.4. Implementation arrangements (max. 1500 words, approximately 3 pages plus diagrams)
NDA and Government Partner
159. Ecuador’s MAATE, as the GCF National Designated Authority (NDA), will ensure that activities implemented by
the project align with strategic national objectives, priorities, and standards, including the National Climate
Change Strategy, and help advance ambitious action on adaptation and mitigation in line with national goals
and needs. The Executing Entity (EE) will engage with the NDA throughout project implementation. The NDA
will contribute to the development of the multiyear workplan and will be provided with detailed reporting on the
status of project activities and impacts. MAATE, through its Undersecretariat of Natural Heritage and
Undersecretariat of Climate Change, will also contribute to project activities and provide grant and in-kind co-
financing as described elsewhere in this proposal.
Accredited Entity
160. CI, through its CI-GCF Agency, will serve as the Accredited Entity (AE) for the project. The CI-GCF Agency will
be responsible for the overall oversight of this project as defined in the Accredited Master Agreement (AMA)
between the GCF and CI, including technical, financial, and administrative monitoring and supervision (through
reporting, audits, and annual site visits) and review and approval of the EE’s annual workplans and budgets.
CI-GCF will also be responsible for providing support, guidance and backstopping to the EE; monitoring the
achievement of project results and Outputs; reporting to the GCF; and project closure and evaluation. CI-GCF
will conduct these responsibilities and disburse GCF funds to the EE. The CI-GCF Agency currently serves as
AE for FP026, Sustainable Landscapes in Madagascar, a GCF project addressing mitigation, adaptation, and
sustainable livelihoods, and for FP158, Ecosystem-based Adaptation and Mitigation in Botswana’s Communal
Rangelands.
Project Governance
161. The project’s governance structure includes the Project Steering Committee and the Project Management
Committee. The Project Steering Committee will be comprised of the following individuals: i) the Undersecretary
of Climate Change of MAATE or his/her delegate; ii) the Undersecretary of Natural Heritage of MAATE or his/her
delegate; and iii) the Vice President of CI-Ecuador or his/her delegate; and will be chaired by the Undersecretary
of Climate Change.
162. The principal functions of the Project Steering Committee will be to provide strategic guidance and support
adaptive management of project implementation, review progress and evaluation reports, discuss problems or
strategic issues that might arise during implementation, and provide support for the necessary inter-institutional
coordination and contributions to project activities. The Steering Committee will also participate in the selection
of the Project Director, through a competitive process.

B.4. Implementation arrangements (max. 1500 words, approximately 3 pages plus diagrams)
NDA and Government Partner
159. Ecuador’s MAATE, as the GCF National Designated Authority (NDA), will ensure that activities implemented by
the project align with strategic national objectives, priorities, and standards, including the National Climate
Change Strategy, and help advance ambitious action on adaptation and mitigation in line with national goals
and needs. The Executing Entity (EE) will engage with the NDA throughout project implementation. The NDA
will contribute to the development of the multiyear workplan and will be provided with detailed reporting on the
status of project activities and impacts. MAATE, through its Undersecretariat of Natural Heritage and
Undersecretariat of Climate Change, will also contribute to project activities and provide grant and in-kind co-
financing as described elsewhere in this proposal.
Accredited Entity
160. CI, through its CI-GCF Agency, will serve as the Accredited Entity (AE) for the project. The CI-GCF Agency will
be responsible for the overall oversight of this project as defined in the Accredited Master Agreement (AMA)
between the GCF and CI, including technical, financial, and administrative monitoring and supervision (through
reporting, audits, and annual site visits) and review and approval of the EE’s annual workplans and budgets.
CI-GCF will also be responsible for providing support, guidance and backstopping to the EE; monitoring the
achievement of project results and Outputs; reporting to the GCF; and project closure and evaluation. CI-GCF
will conduct these responsibilities and disburse GCF funds to the EE. The CI-GCF Agency currently serves as
AE for FP026, Sustainable Landscapes in Madagascar, a GCF project addressing mitigation, adaptation, and
sustainable livelihoods, and for FP158, Ecosystem-based Adaptation and Mitigation in Botswana’s Communal
Rangelands.
Project Governance
161. The project’s governance structure includes the Project Steering Committee and the Project Management
Committee. The Project Steering Committee will be comprised of the following individuals: i) the Undersecretary
of Climate Change of MAATE or his/her delegate; ii) the Undersecretary of Natural Heritage of MAATE or his/her
delegate; and iii) the Vice President of CI-Ecuador or his/her delegate; and will be chaired by the Undersecretary
of Climate Change.
162. The principal functions of the Project Steering Committee will be to provide strategic guidance and support
adaptive management of project implementation, review progress and evaluation reports, discuss problems or
strategic issues that might arise during implementation, and provide support for the necessary inter-institutional
coordination and contributions to project activities. The Steering Committee will also participate in the selection
of the Project Director, through a competitive process.

163. The Project Steering Committee will meet at least twice per year, to review the progress of the ongoing semester
or year and to advise the Project Director on strategic and policy-related decisions. The Project Steering
Committee will be convened by the Project Director in advance to give the members sufficient time to schedule
the meeting and agree on the agenda. The Project Director will prepare minutes of each meeting. Extraordinary
meetings of the Project Steering Committee will be convened when deemed necessary and at the request of
one of its members. The Project Steering Committee may also invite key stakeholders to support specific
themes.
164. The Project Management Advisory Committee will comprise the following individuals: i) the Director of the
Marine, Coastal and Oceanic50 (MAATE); ii) the Director of Climate Change Adaptation51 (MAATE); iii) a
representative from VMAP; d) the Director of the Coastal and Marine Program of CI-Ecuador; and e) the Project
Directorr employed by CI; and will be chaired by the Director of Marine, Coastal and Oceanic from MAATE.
165. The Project Management Advisory Committee will meet at least four times per year to advise the Project
Director and the National Project Director (NPD) on technical matters, and to discuss challenges and
collaboration opportunities during implementation. The Project Management Committee may invite key partners
to provide advice on specific themes.
166. For both governance committees, members who are government employees will not be renumerated by GCF
funds.
167. The NPD will be appointed by MAATE’s Undersecretary of Natural Heritage, who will be employed and funded
by MAATE. The NPD will advise the Project Director on government policies and priorities; review coherence
of the project activities, including results, risks, planning and procurement processes; advise on the project’s
annual Procurement Plan for project services and goods; and review the technical and financial quarterly project
reports to the AE.
168. Technical Advisory Committees will be created for each of the Components and each of the estuaries of the
project. In the case of Component 1, the Technical Advisory Committee will include MAATE, the Risk
Secretariat, Universities working in climate change adaptation and mitigation, and representatives from
AUSCEM. The Component 2 committee will include MAATE, the VMAP, the NCA, the Fisheries and
Aquaculture Public Research Institute, shrimp farmers, and other value chain members. The Component 3
committee will include MAATE, the VMAP, the Planning National Secretariat, Provincial, municipal, and Parish
governments, and representatives of the INOCAR, the Prosecutor’s Office, and Judiciary Council. At the level
of each estuary, a committee will be created to integrate the Zonal Directors of MAATE with the planning
activities of local governments and AUSCEMS representatives.
Executing Entity
169. CI will self-execute this project. CI, acting through its country office in Ecuador (referred to in this proposal as
“CI” or “CI-Ecuador”),52 will be the EE for all activities of this project. CI-Ecuador will be responsible for project
execution, management of sub-grantees and their activities, reporting to the AE, and ensuring optimal alignment
with the Government of Ecuador policies and priorities in coordination with MAATE to achieve project outcomes
and Fund-level goals. As EE, CI, acting through its country office in Ecuador, will enter into legal agreements
(including sub-grant agreements, services agreements, and MOUs) for this project. As a branch of CI, CI-
Ecuador will follow CI policies and procedures in the execution of this project, including terms included in CI’s
Operations Manual (see Annex 21). The CI-GCF Agency has assessed the capacity of CI-Ecuador and has
determined it to be capable of applying CI and CI-GCF standards and policies in the execution of this Project.
Throughout Project implementation, CI-Ecuador will be supported by various CI divisions which will lend specific
expertise, including CI’s Americas Division, Conservation Finance Division (supporting private-sector funding),

and CI’s Center for Oceans (supporting blue carbon and mangrove restoration), and Project Delivery and

170. For more than 30 years, CI has been protecting nature for the benefit of all. CI employs more than 1,000 people
and works with more than 2,000 partners in 30+ countries. Since 1987, CI has supported more than 1,200 PAs
and undertaken interventions across 77 countries, protecting more than 601 million hectares of land, marine
and coastal areas. For over two decades, CI has been operating in Ecuador in the Galápagos, marine and
coastal areas of continental Ecuador, Andes Choco and the Amazon to implement conservation solutions within
priority landscapes. CI has supported the creation and management of marine and coastal PAs, working in
close collaboration with artisanal fisher associations to promote capacity building and sustainable management
measures of resources (in Galápagos with the spiny lobster, tuna, and prawn fisheries and in mainland Ecuador,
with black shell and red crab fisheries).
171. CI-Ecuador will establish the main Project Management Unit (PMU) at CI’s office in Guayaquil. The PMU will
be headed by a full-time Project Director employed by CI, who will be responsible for coordination with all
stakeholders and successful implementation of the project and attainment of results specified in the project’s
Funding Proposal, to the required standards of quality and within the specified constraints of time and cost. The
PMU will be responsible for overall project management and planning, providing support to the execution of
day-to-day activities, coordinating with the national government and project partners, coordinating with the AE,
managing and overseeing grants, and coordinating project execution across two project offices and four estuary
sites. The PMU will also include the Operations and Finance Director and the Project Finance Manager and will
receive support from the CI-Ecuador Senior Operations Director. Upon project inception, the Project Director in
coordination with the Operations and Finance Director will prepare a Project Operations Manual, including
responsibilities, procedures and details for a smooth and effective implementation, which will be approved by
the Project Steering Committee. The project will also have dedicated full-time staff, including Monitoring &
Evaluation Manager, Safeguards Manager, Gender Manager, Communications & Knowledge Manager,
Procurement Manager and 3 Component Leads. Additional Project staff as detailed in Annex 4 will be based
in Guayaquil and Esmeraldas.
172. In addition, CI, in coordination with MAATE, will establish an Estuary Field Unit (EFU) in each of the Project’s
four target estuaries (Cayapas-Mataje, Muisne-Cojimíes, Gulf of Guayaquil, and Jambelí) as regional execution
offices. These EFUs will be housed in the MAATE Provincial Directions in Esmeraldas, Guayaquil, and Machala
(as in-kind support from MAATE), and will each be led by two project staff: an Estuary Coordinator and a Social
Technician (CI staff for the southern estuaries and PUSECE staff for the northern estuaries). Establishing EFUs
in the estuaries directly involved in the day-to-day activities of the local associations is a cost-effective strategy
for achieving the goals of the project. Evaluations on mangrove areas have shown that frequent technical
support leads to sustainable change in behavior and practices in local communities to change behavior and
practices. The Estuary Coordinator will ensure effective liaison and coordination with local stakeholders and
local government, Component Leads, the PMU, and the other EFUs in implementation of the project activities,
and the supporting staff will support implementation of Conservation and Stewardship Agreements with fishing
associations and in-kind grants to local NGOs and universities.

174. PUCESE will execute activities under Component 1 in the two northern estuaries in the project: Cayapas Mataje
and Muisne Cojimies. PUCESE has prior experience working with local communities and local associations that
depend on mangrove resources as well as developing research on mangroves and climate change. PUCESE
is based close to the project area and has opened a new campus near the target Cayapas Mataje estuary. CI
will enter into a cash grant agreement with PUCESE for the implementation of its designated activities in
Component 1. CI Ecuador will apply risk mitigation measures and manage and monitor in-kind sub-grants to
ensure that goods are used in a manner consistent with CI and GCF policies, including safeguards and
prohibited practices.
175. FIAS manages the Socio Manglar subaccount of the existing Socio Bosque Fund. Under Output 2.2., CI will
provide a $4 million grant to FIAS that FIAS will hold in an interest-bearing account and use the interest
generated to pay for community incentive payments under the Socio Manglar program. FIAS is a private, non-
profit entity that has a track record of the management, mobilization, investment and implementation of public
and private funds to finance conservation of natural resources and biodiversity, mitigation and adaptation to
climate change and environmental quality, complementing the efforts of the Government of Ecuador. FIAS
currently has more than US$100 million in five funds under management, with contributions from the
Government of Ecuador, Germany, Italy and other international sources, including GCF. CI has contributed to
three FIAS funds to date, including a US$4 million in grant funds for the Ecuador Azul Fund managed by FIAS.
176. CI will also enter into sub-grant agreements with community AUSCEM associations as described under
Component 1 (selection criteria in Annex 2, Appendix 5). CI will provide goods via in-kind grants to the
associations that follow the terms of related conservation agreements between CI and the associations for
conservation, management and restoration activities as described under Activity 1.1.1. CI will also enter into
small cash grant agreements with the community associations for the development of enterprises and livelihood
activities as described under Activity 1.2.2.

B.5. Justification for GCF funding request (max. 1000 words, approximately 2 pages)
181. Public and private funding for the activities proposed in this project are limited and insufficient to tackle the
ongoing challenge of mangrove deforestation and restoration at the scale required. GCF funding is necessary
to implement the project and catalyze change. Project beneficiaries are among the most vulnerable populations
in Ecuador, with direct exposure to heightened flood risks and livelihood disruptions associated with climate
change compounded by low adaptive capacity due to poverty.
182. Grant funding is warranted to create the enabling conditions for sustained capacity and investment. The
important ecosystem services that mangroves provide are not currently adequately valued by public and private
sector actors and receive limited investment. In particular, mangroves have proven effectiveness in reducing

B.5. Justification for GCF funding request (max. 1000 words, approximately 2 pages)
181. Public and private funding for the activities proposed in this project are limited and insufficient to tackle the
ongoing challenge of mangrove deforestation and restoration at the scale required. GCF funding is necessary
to implement the project and catalyze change. Project beneficiaries are among the most vulnerable populations
in Ecuador, with direct exposure to heightened flood risks and livelihood disruptions associated with climate
change compounded by low adaptive capacity due to poverty.
182. Grant funding is warranted to create the enabling conditions for sustained capacity and investment. The
important ecosystem services that mangroves provide are not currently adequately valued by public and private
sector actors and receive limited investment. In particular, mangroves have proven effectiveness in reducing

=== PAGE 56 ===
GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0 | PAGE 49
B
escalating coastal flood risks due to climate change and they are among the world’s most valuable ecosystems
for mitigating GHG emissions, especially through the accumulation of carbon in soil as organic matter.
183. Gaps in the generation and dissemination of data regarding climate change scenarios and mangrove ecosystem
services for adaptation in coastal Ecuador have resulted in public entities, particularly subnational governments
(provincial, municipal and parishes), not adequately incorporating climate change risks and the
protection/restoration of mangroves into investment decisions. Similarly, while the carbon sequestration benefits
of mangroves, particularly in soils, are well documented globally, better Ecuador-specific data and monitoring
protocols to integrate these climate benefits fully into national public investment, mitigation strategies and GHG
accounting are needed to better mobilize both national and international finance. These gaps have historically
led to under-investment in mangrove conservation and restoration in Ecuador, despite the strong public benefits
of mangroves.
184. Private-sector investment in mangroves has also been constrained. Private sector investment would in principle
be motivated by: i) the potential for private returns; ii) regulatory requirements; and iii) market drivers (e.g.
demand for sustainable shrimp). To date, these factors have had limited effects in driving private investment,
and the project seeks to address these constraints.
185. Private sector returns from mangrove conservation and restoration have been undervalued, leading in the
aquaculture sector to extensive production models that have driven approximately half of mangrove
deforestation in Ecuador historically. There is, however, strong potential for key actors to internalize benefits
through reduced costs and/or increased revenues. Shrimp aquaculture, using CSS models (see section B.3
and Annex 2), can increase yields, profitability and resilience through sustainable intensification, including
increases in on-farm mangrove areas, requiring a shift away from Ecuador’s traditional, extensive methods of
shrimp aquaculture. Shifting a critical mass of early movers to CSS will leverage additional private finance and
drive a broader sector-wide change in practice.
186. For community associations linked to protection of mangroves, reinforcing and expanding enterprises and
revenue streams such as crab and shellfish fisheries or ecotourism also provide a way to internalize the returns
of their mangrove conservation commitments under stewardship agreements (AUSCEM, see section B.3 and
Annex 2). Currently, these livelihoods and income generating activities are not optimized, limiting the scale and
effectiveness of these conservation strategies.
187. Regulatory requirements have contributed to reversing Ecuador’s mangrove deforestation trends, but gross
deforestation continues due to insufficient investment in enforcement and oversight, while market signals,
especially from international demand for more sustainable products in shrimp markets, are rapidly
strengthening.
188. Motivating private sector investment, which has to date been marginal for mangrove conservation/restoration
requires better alignment of the potential for private benefits and regulatory frameworks and incentives that
recognize the public benefits and externalities of mangroves. By generating and providing data, facilitating
technical support and building the capacity of both the public and private sectors, the project will support long-
term integration into policy and institutional frameworks governing mangroves as well as strengthening the
financial mechanisms for stable investment flows.
189. The project objectives align well with GCF’s function as demonstrated by its contribution to three of GCF’s
priority results areas (forestry and land-use, most vulnerable people and communities and ecosystems and
ecosystem services) and the significant GHG emissions reductions and adaptation benefits it will provide. Given
the existing challenges for Ecuador’s government budget, which have been exacerbated by the economic
impacts of Covid, significant national public funding for the project is not realistic. Nevertheless, the project will
benefit from some government co-financing for support to community mangrove conservation. The project
provides an important opportunity to catalyze current interest in improving sustainability within the shrimp
farming industry such that it adopts a pathway towards zero mangrove deforestation and mangrove restoration,
which will provide important climate change mitigation and coastal flood protection benefits. Further, there is a
substantial unrealized but demonstrated opportunity to protect mangroves through community conservation,
which would strengthen protection of existing areas and provide opportunities for restoration while also creating
new economic opportunities for some of Ecuador’s poorest and most vulnerable coastal communities. However,
to capitalize on these opportunities, grant funding is critical to overcome the barriers to mangrove conservation
and restoration. CI has raised funding for successful pilot projects on community conservation and promoting

=== PAGE 57 ===
GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0 | PAGE 50
B
climate-smart shrimp, but these are insufficient to tackle the issues for mangroves at the scale required. GCF
funding for this project would not be duplicating work supported by other donors.
B.6. Exit strategy (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)
[See Annex 2, Feasibility Study for more details.]
190. Project investments will drive a paradigm shift in the institutional, organizational and financial frameworks
governing mangroves as a climate-change adaptation and mitigation tool, by the private and public sector. The
project includes various activities that build sustainability, outlined below by component.
Project Component 1: Mangrove areas under effective and climate-adapted management increased,
including through community-based management (AUSCEMs) and PAs implementing climate adaptation
plans.
191. Project investments in strengthening the management of AUSCEMs and PAs, expanding their coverage, and
explicitly incorporating climate risks and benefits into management will consolidate a foundation that will be
institutionalized and extend beyond the project lifespan.
192. AUSCEMs and Socio Manglar agreements create legally binding terms and agreements for community-based
mangrove conservation and incentives for up to two decades. Project investments will support training, planning
and administrative support to overcome initial establishment costs for agreements and thus scale up long-
lasting coverage of these programs.
193. AUSCEMs establish (renewable) ten-year commitments from communities (with contracts established
voluntarily and with mutual consent, between the government and community beneficiaries) to conserve and
sustainably manage mangroves, recognized and enforced by the environmental authority. Areas restored to
mangroves under this component will be within AUSCEMs or within formal PAs to ensure continued protection
of these areas beyond project implementation. The project design assumes that the Government of Ecuador
will continue to use AUSCEMs as a mechanism for engaging communities in mangrove conservation and
sustainable management.
194. The project will expand the coverage of Socio Manglar agreements to cover an additional 20,000 ha. These
Socio Manglar agreements embody a 10-year renewable commitment on the part of the Government of Ecuador
to sustain these areas and benefits, while including additional legal provisions in their contracts beyond the
terms of AUSCEMs, including financial planning, accounting, tax obligations and reporting to the environmental
authority and to the members. As noted in the project Theory of Change, an important assumption of the project
is that the government continues to be committed to the use of AUSCEMs and Socio Manglar agreements.
195. The project also includes incorporating climate adaptation into the strategies of government-run mangrove-
dominated Marine Protected Areas (MPAs). By integrating these strategies into the management plans of these
MPAs, any long-term costs associated with them will be integrated into the regular MPA budgets. Ecuador’s
MPAs already have consistent funding annually from the government but there are also additional ongoing
actions to increase the sustainability of funding for the MPAs through two dedicated trust funds. This work on
MPA sustainable funding is not directly part of this GCF project but will contribute to the long-term sustainability
of the GCF investments.
196. Legal commitments and financial commitments will contribute to reinforcing a solid foundation of grassroots
capacity built by the project. Social capital and local livelihoods require legal and financial instruments, and vice
versa. Mangrove-based associations already have a strong predisposition and interest in mangrove
conservation, driving the signing of AUSCEM agreements covering 60,000 ha to date. By investing in
organizational and planning capacity across 60 associations with AUSCEMs, the project will strengthen human
capacity of these local organizations and PA staff to value benefits of mangroves, including climate resilience,
based on a participatory, gender-sensitive approach. Further reinforcing local commitments to mangrove
management, local enterprises will perceive increased economic benefits through the implementation of
business plans and strategies that are economically viable and have continuity after the project ends.

--- Table 1 on Page 57 ---
climate-smart shrimp, but these are insufficient to tackle the issues for mangroves at the scale required. GCF
funding for this project would not be duplicating work supported by other donors.
B.6. Exit strategy (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)
[See Annex 2, Feasibility Study for more details.]

=== PAGE 58 ===
GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0 | PAGE 51
B
Project Component 2: The private sector becomes a transformational agent for change by reducing GHG
emissions and providing financial support to conserve and restore mangroves that increase climate
resilience for other coastal populations.
197. Long-term sustainability is the underlying rationale for this project outcome. By recognizing and internalizing
the value of mangrove conservation and restoration in private-sector practices and investment decisions, the
project will lay the foundation for ongoing action.
198. In the shrimp aquaculture sector, shifting a significant proportion of production area to climate-smart practices
will lead to on-farm improvements in mangrove conservation and other climate-related practices over 20,000
ha of aquaculture. By linking these actions by early adopters to public-private dialogue and market engagement,
the project aims to shift policies, commercial strategies and common practice broadly across the sector,
positioning Ecuador’s shrimp sector internationally and driving demand and therefore further adoption of
ASC/SSP certification and CSS practices. Once established as common, profitable practices and demand
grows, any ongoing investment to maintain ASC/SSP certification status and CSS practices is expected to come
from the companies themselves, either through self-financing made possible by enhanced profitability or from
private financial institutions (see activity 2.1.2). In addition, in parallel with this project, CI is working with partners
to establish a Climate Smart Shrimp Fund that will create a track record of successful CSS loans (as defined
by loan repayment at commercial rate5s3, farm profitability, and mangrove restoration) to demonstrate
attractiveness to local financial institutions.
199. The CSS Fund is being developed as a blended finance vehicle, with approximately 30% concessional finance
and 70% commercial finance. The fund's long-term goal is to make the case to local financial institutions that
they can and should make CSS loans. Although the CSS Fund is not being developed as part of this GCF-
funded project, it will contribute to the sustainability of the work being supported by GCF. Should the CSS Fund
be fully capitalized during project implementation, this funding will be considered parallel financing.
200. Certification schemes are designed to reward responsible farming practices. Globally, buyers in major markets
are making strong commitments to purchase farmed seafood products from certified sources and ASC is a
recognized leader in seafood certification. This growing demand provides the incentive needed for shrimp farms
to invest in more sustainable practices and to undergo the assessments necessary to obtain certification. The
project will remove the knowledge barriers that are currently blocking more widespread participation in
certification and by the end of the project there will be more examples of small- and medium-sized farms that
have obtained private financing that will serve as examples for the industry and for the financial institutions that
invest. The market-based incentives created by certification, and the influence of best practice among early
adopters, combined with improved regulation (through the activities of Component 3) should mean that more
attention is paid to environmental concerns including ensuring no further clearing of mangrove beyond the
project. Price premiums for certified shrimp can be elusive. Instead, market access via preferential sourcing is
a common incentive for farms to attain certification54. The project will support farmers to access these markets.
201. The project will expand a dedicated long-term finance mechanism to assure continuity of resources for the
Socio Manglar incentive payments to communities managing mangroves, and leverage commitments from the
Government of Ecuador, which will provide US$449,000 per year for these incentives as cofinance. The project
will contribute to capitalizing the Socio Manglar subaccount of the existing and highly successful Socio Bosque
Fund. Four million dollars (US$4 million) of GCF funding is requested to increase the capital of the Socio
Manglar subaccount, which will leverage additional private investment including from companies involved in
shrimp aquaculture and Ecuador’s PECC. The GCF contribution of $4 million will be held in an interest-bearing
bank account while funds from other contributors will be held in an investment account and invested in
accordance with the investment policy of FIAS. Projections for the growth of the capitalization and use of the
subaccount are provided in Appendix 12 of Annex 2. By clearly demonstrating and documenting emissions
mitigation benefits, the project will also contribute to creating the necessary conditions for results-based
payments (RBPs) for mangrove conservation in the long-term.
53 https://www.climatefinancelab.org/ideas/climate-smart-shrimp-fund/
54 For more information on markets of certified shrimp, see FS section 4.6.1.3 and From Company Commitments to
Collective Global Action: What’s Next for our Sustainable Seafood Strategy • The David and Lucile Packard Foundation

=== PAGE 59 ===
GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0 | PAGE 52
B
Project Component 3: Create the enabling conditions for sustaining reductions in mangrove deforestation
and increased mangrove restoration by strengthening governance, climate change adaptation strategies,
coastal management policies, and legal enforcement.
202. Sustainability and long-term permanence of improvements to policies and public institutions will be ensured by
a combination of legal/regulatory instruments and institutional capacity.
203. A key set of outputs of this Component relate to the inclusive development of policy, planning and legal
instruments that will govern management, enforcement and government budget allocations. These instruments
will extend beyond the end of GCF funding and will include instruments at various levels:
• GADs will have codified mangrove conservation and climate considerations in local development plans,
regulations and policy frameworks (e.g. municipal ordinances, budgets, PDOTs, COA, integrated coastal
development plans), ensuring the basis for long-term application of these regulatory instruments guiding
development and public investment.
• Harmonized public policy instruments for the management of the mangrove ecosystem (e.g., procedures
for assuring transparency in regulation, decision making and enforcement action, inter-agency
cooperation for establishing technical standards).
• Improvements in procedures at MAATE for reception and processing of complaints, enforcement and
sanctions, reflected in regulatory reforms formally adopted by the Ministry and/or other public entities.
204. In addition, capacity building for national and regional institutions will help ensure that institutions can continue
to monitor the effectiveness and sustainability of their actions after project completion and improve their
effectiveness. Key partners in project implementation have been, and will be, involved in all aspects of project
design and execution to ensure that they can sustain these activities after the GCF funding concludes. For
example, forest monitoring data, specific to mangroves, including robust accounting of soil organic carbon, will
be generated by the project with the aim of including a robust set of data and methodologies for inclusion by
Ecuador´s MAATE in the national Forest Reference Emissions Level (FREL). By incorporating this very
significant carbon pool and sequestration into national monitoring, Ecuador will be able to provide a more
comprehensive accounting of emissions and reductions as well as potentially mobilizing additional carbon
finance. Also, institutionalization of training programs, curricula and materials developed by the project will
provide the basis for ongoing replication by MAATE and the Judicial Branch after the project concludes.

GCF Funding is requested for all Project Activities. In addition, co-financing will be provided by the Government of
Ecuador, through MAATE, and CI. MAATE will provide in-kind staff time and associated operational cost to support work
under Output 1.1 (see paragraphs 113 and 184). Staff time will include MAATE specialists in climate change, sustainable
production and development, forestry direction, and PAs as well as regional personnel. Operational costs associated
with the staff costs (e.g., rent, communications, services, supplies and maintenance) will also be provided in-kind.
MAATE will also provide in-kind staff time and associated operational costs to support its PMC-related activities. MAATE
will also provide grant funding in the form of direct incentive payments to beneficiaries for the Socio Manglar program
contributing to A1.1.1 impacts.
CI will provide in-kind financing of indirect costs that are not eligible for GCF financing to support all components of this
project (see Annex 14 for details).

emphasis on women, youth and other vulnerable groups (Activities 1.1.1 and Output 1.2; GCF funding requested
US$561,675)
Shrimp famers: Increasing the technical capacity of small- and medium-sized shrimp farmers to drive adoption of
climate-smart practices (Project Output 2.1; GCF funding requested US$289,390) is key to the success of the project.
Government:
• MAATE’s capacities for planning and implementation of activities that improve climate resilience and mangrove
conservation in PAs will also be improved (Activity 1.1.2; GCF funding requested US$82,266);
• National entities (MAATE, judiciary) will attend training and technical assistance for complaints, enforcement
and sanctions for infractions affecting mangroves (Activity 3.2.2; GCF funding requested US$230,774).
• Government entities will have the capacity to generate and incorporate improved mangrove forest cover and
carbon stock data for planning, policies, investments and enforcement through training and basic equipment
(Activity 3.1.1; GCF funding requested US$12,058);
• Local and provincial governments (GADs) will have the capacity to develop, update and/or adopt zoning and
development plans including criteria and indicators relating to mangrove protection, climate change mitigation
or adaptation (Activity 3.2.1; GCF funding requested US$39,884);
Local NGOs and universities: The Project will also support the capacity building of partners that need to enforce their
operation practices through Enforcement Plans to subgrantees. There will be a technology transfer in restoration
practices with green-gray practices in Ecuador through the trainings provided to shrimp farmers and promotion of the
CSS concept (Project Output 2.1).

=== PAGE 64 ===
GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0 | PAGE 57
D
EXPECTED PERFORMANCE AGAINST INVESTMENT CRITERIA
This section refers to the performance of the project/programme against the investment criteria as set out in the
GCF’s Initial Investment Framework.
D.1. Impact potential (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)
205. The project will provide both adaptation and mitigation benefits. The direct adaptation benefits come from
reduced flooding risk of vulnerable people and project activities that are expected to increase people’s
resilience through livelihood benefits associated with better mangrove management. The mitigation
benefits come from a combination of reduced emissions by reducing mangrove deforestation and from
carbon sequestration in areas of restored mangroves.
206. Eight priority municipalities, with a population of 3.4 million, were selected based on mangrove cover and
population at risk of flooding related to climate change (see B.1.1.4). Flood risk modelling including the
effect of mangrove cover to reduce risk was used to estimate flood risk beneficiaries and expected reduced
losses of economic assets (see Annex 25). Based on the modelling, the flood risk for 89,600 people can
be mitigated by reducing mangrove loss and enhancing mangrove restoration efforts to increase the rate
of mangrove recovery as compared to baseline rates. The population at risk is likely to increase as
population grows, however we conservatively assume benefits for the 89,600 people as modelling
population growth and migration introduces great uncertainty into the estimations (See Annex 25 for details
of Adaptation beneficiaries).

Mitigation Impacts
• GHG emissions from gross deforestation under the baseline scenario are projected to be 2,428,986 tCO2e
over the project implementation period and 11,062,246 tCO2e over 20 years.
• Reductions in GHG emissions from reducing deforestation due to project interventions relative to this
baseline scenario are projected to be 567,404 tCO2e over the project period and 3,566,685 tCO2e over
20 years.
• In addition, removals of GHG emissions from project reforestation activities are projected to be 164,707
tCO2e over the project implementation and 1,042,785 tCO2e over 20 years.
• Total net GHG reductions, from avoided deforestation and mangrove restoration are projected to be
732,111 tCO2e over the project implementation period and 4,609,470 tCO2e over 20 years.
• A detailed description of methods and data is included in Annex 22 along with an Excel workbook with
the calculations of GHG emission reductions, including all assumptions and equations used, to enable a
technical assessment to retrace the calculations.
• Estimates will be refined through on-the-ground measurements during project implementation.

20 years
207. In addition to the project’s contributions towards advancing national climate change adaption and mitigation
plans, the project will also contribute to the aims of the GMA57, which brings together a global community of
practitioners to halt mangrove loss, restore lost mangroves and secure mangrove protection. CI is a founding
member along with other international organizations and the project will contribute in two ways to the GMA’s
work.
208. The first relates to ongoing knowledge sharing of GMA, especially related to mangrove restoration and “blue
carbon” information. One of the functions of GMA is as a knowledge hub to share information between
practitioners engaged in mangrove conservation and restoration. The mangrove restoration approach is
informed by GMA toolkits and best practice guides. Data collected under activity 3.1.1 will also be shared
with the GMA as a matter of course (through the Mangrove Restoration Tracker Tool and Global Mangrove
Watch).
209. Second, the project itself makes an important contribution to the ‘Mangrove Breakthrough’, an initiative
launched by the GMA and the UN Climate Change high level Champions during the UNFCCC COP27. This
high-level initiative aims to secure the future of 15 million hectares of mangroves globally (through
conservation, restoration and securing long-term financing of mangrove protection). This project alone
represents about 1% of this goal.
57 https://www.mangrovealliance.org/

D.2. Paradigm shift potential (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)
210. This project will drive a paradigm shift in the way that Ecuador values and benefits from critical natural
infrastructure that protects coastal people and assets from flooding. By fully valuing the adaptation and
mitigation benefits of mangrove ecosystems and integrating these into governance and economic
frameworks across Ecuador’s coastal region, the project presents an innovative approach to the goals of
reducing GHG emissions and improving the livelihoods of some of the country's most vulnerable people.
The current paradigm undervalues both climate risks and the ecosystem services provided by mangroves,
particularly with regards to climate change adaptation and mitigation. Under the current paradigm, exposure
to climate risk and GHG emissions are both augmented by lack of sufficient government, private sector and
civil society action. This situation is due to lack of adequate information, institutional coordination,
empowerment of local stakeholders, and mobilization of private sector investment.
211. The combination of bolstering public sector frameworks and capacity, catalyzing public-private partnerships
and private investment and strengthening local community participation and economic benefits, with a focus
on the ‘green infrastructure’ that mangroves provide is an innovative and cost-effective approach with strong
potential for scaling up and replication. Successful approaches will be sustained by local communities and
government entities over time and the project will create lessons, guidance and tools for application in other
countries with communities living in coastal areas threatened by climate change.
212. Across all activities, the project will engage in comprehensive monitoring and reporting with the aim of
strengthening learning and knowledge sharing. The project aims to carry out two comprehensive
evaluations: at the project mid-term and at the final evaluation of the project. Each exercise will include the
identification of lessons learned and their dissemination.
213. Complementing the strong focus on local action, community participation, gender equity and private sector
engagement, the project will consistently work to integrate these into a strong enabling environment and
policy framework. This enabling environment includes the strengthening of regulatory and policy frameworks
at the national and subnational level, as well as creating mechanisms and successful ongoing processes for
dialogue and collaboration across sectors.
214. The project includes a series of capacity building packages for local communities to strengthen their ability
to participate in innovative business models, as well as to engage more closely in the land use planning
process with local government. Building consensus among key stakeholders, including government,
community groups, and private sector actors, is a difficult task, largely neglected because of its high
transaction costs. However, many experiences confirm that sharing a common vision is a very efficient and
effective mechanism through which to achieve social objectives. The project approach is to develop a
pragmatic mechanism through which consensus can be reached on the operational management of
mangroves. The project has selected several planning tools to be formulated through a transparent and
open participatory process, guided by the entity responsible for their promulgation and facilitated through
the project. Technical assistance, training, logistical support, and coordination are the responsibilities of the
project, along with the facilitation process to allow all stakeholders to express their views, expectations, and
contributions, and to reach agreements for the benefit of all. It is expected that after several similar
participatory processes, community leaders, public officials, and private sector leaders will learn that such
an approach is valid and worthwhile, despite the additional initial transaction costs, and that reaching
agreements is an effective means of managing relationships with other stakeholders.
215. The project will strengthen the participation of the private sector through its support to the definition of clear
land use plans and the adoption of climate-smart practices, especially conservation and restoration of
mangroves.
216. The project involves the scaling up and consolidation of activities that have successfully been used to
conserve and restore mangroves in Ecuador. The area where the project is located lacks the most basic
land-use planning instruments that could guide the sustainable use of natural resources. These instruments
will be built jointly between the local communities and the local government as a first step to ensure the
long-term sustainable development of the provinces involved. The project will facilitate participatory, open
and transparent planning processes. In addition, the participatory process for land use planning will be
closely documented and the lessons from this experience will be shared with the national government.
Legal and regulatory adjustments, as needed for effective planning, will be recommended through technical
documents.

D.3. Sustainable development (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)
Environmental co-benefits
217. The project will reduce deforestation, increase restoration and enhance management across Ecuador’s
entire mangrove area of 156,633 ha.
218. In addition to the mitigation and flood-risk abatement benefits highlighted under GCF’s reporting framework
(D.1), mangroves provide significant additional ecosystem services including biodiversity conservation and
habitats for fishery nursing grounds. Ecuadorian mangroves are home to 179 plant species (Cornejo, 2014)
and constitute important nesting grounds for numerous bird species. Mangroves are also an important
habitat for spawning, breeding and nurseries of commercial and non-commercial fish species. The
structural complexity and ecological conditions of mangroves provide both protection from predators and
abundant food resources due to the high primary productivity of this ecosystem (Huxham et al., 2004,
Mirera et al., 2010, Shinnaka et al., 2007, Wang et al., 2009). Globally, secondary production of fish is 1-
11.8 t/ha/y (Mirera, Kairo, and Waweru, 2010). Fisheries production dependent on mangroves alone has
been estimated to be US$100-21,000/ha/y (Spalding et al, 2010). According to UNEP (2014), the full range
of ecosystem services are worth an average US$33,000 to US$57,000/ha/yr to the national economies of
developing countries with mangroves (UNEP, 2014). A more recent review of 250 studies around the world
that have estimated the biodiversity and ecosystem service value of one hectare of mangrove reported an
average value of US$21,100 ha/yr in 2018 prices (Getzner et al., 2020). Irrespective of valuation methods
used, the importance of mangrove fisheries as a food source for local communities is widely recognized.
Social co-benefits including health impacts
219. In addition to the reduction of flood risks, the project will result in additional co-benefits, especially for
mangrove-dependent communities and associations:
• Improved social cohesion and organizational capacity for mangrove management by 60 associations
will lead to enhanced ability to engage with government stakeholders, resolve internal conflicts and
develop additional community activities.
• Improved mangrove management and plans are likely to integrate other social and environmental
considerations with benefits for human health and wellbeing including water management and waste
disposal.
• Improvements in control of illegal activities in mangrove areas, in partnership between community-
based associations and government authorities will also generate collateral benefits in enhancing
local security.
Economic co-benefits
220. Mangrove conservation and restoration is a relatively low-cost solution for climate change adaptation that
will generate economic benefits for the government, the private sector, and the communities through the
avoided cost of the impacts of extreme climate events. The shrimp sector will be able to access new
markets by producing shrimp more sustainably and being able to brand and market the product
accordingly, resulting in increased export revenue and employment.
221. Communities will see lower flooding and relocation costs, reduced impacts on infrastructure, housing,
crops and livelihoods. Livelihood diversification and adaptive fishing techniques will increase the
resilience, economic stability, and food security of marginalized coastal fishing communities. Mangrove
ecosystems are the foundation for diversified local economies, with 80% of the human population of the
mangrove ecosystem dedicated to artisanal fishing, obtaining corvina (Cynoscion albus), mullet (Mugil
curema), sea catfish (Arius kesslei) and grunts (Anisotremus caesius), as well as red crab (Ucides
occidentalis) and black clam (Anadara spp.). Several of these species migrate to the sea and are an
important contribution to the composition of rich marine fisheries, frequently exploited by the marine fishing
industry.
Gender-sensitive development impact
222. The project will contribute directly to increasing the empowerment of women and other vulnerable groups
in economic activities and advancing their participation and leadership in organizations and enterprises.
See G.2 and Annex 8.

 | 221. Communities will see lower flooding and relocation costs, reduced impacts on infrastructure, housing, | 
 | crops and livelihoods. Livelihood diversification and adaptive fishing techniques will increase the | 
 | resilience, economic stability, and food security of marginalized coastal fishing communities. Mangrove | 
 | ecosystems are the foundation for diversified local economies, with 80% of the human population of the | 
 | mangrove ecosystem dedicated to artisanal fishing, obtaining corvina (Cynoscion albus), mullet (Mugil | 
 | curema), sea catfish (Arius kesslei) and grunts (Anisotremus caesius), as well as red crab (Ucides | 
 | occidentalis) and black clam (Anadara spp.). Several of these species migrate to the sea and are an | 
 | important contribution to the composition of rich marine fisheries, frequently exploited by the marine fishing | 
 | industry. | 
 |  | 
 | Gender-sensitive development impact | 
 | 222. The project will contribute directly to increasing the empowerment of women and other vulnerable groups | 
 | in economic activities and advancing their participation and leadership in organizations and enterprises. | 
 | See G.2 and Annex 8. |

=== PAGE 68 ===
GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0 | PAGE 61
D
D.4. Needs of recipient (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)
223. Economic growth in Ecuador has directly reduced poverty and inequality levels and increased the size of
the middle class. Between 2007 and 2018, poverty decreased from 36.7% to 23.2%, while extreme poverty
decreased from 16.5% to 8.4%. Similarly, inequality as measured by the GINI Coefficient has fallen from
0.55 in 2007 to 0.47 in 2018.
224. However, the economy is still heavily dependent on the oil sector, a vulnerability demonstrated in recent
years during periods of low oil prices and the appreciation of the US dollar. The reduction of this
dependency has been part of the national strategies implemented by the central government.
225. With more than 3,630 km of coastline, approximately 54% of Ecuador's population of 17 million people lives
in Ecuador's five coastal provinces. These areas are particularly vulnerable to SLR and the associated risks
of saltwater intrusion and storm surges, flooding and storms.
226. These trends put coastal areas in Ecuador at particular risk. With 45% of the population below the extreme
poverty line in the project area, the poverty rate in coastal areas is well above the national average. An
increasing number of predominantly poor and vulnerable people in the coastal areas live in unsafe housing,
in part due to rapid urbanization and persistent poverty.
227. Beyond the basic challenges of coastal development, the risks of climate change are placing additional
stresses on the Government of Ecuador's efforts to achieve sustainable development. Despite the high
level of exposure and vulnerability of the country's coasts, there is no integrated plan for the protection of
mangroves and wetlands to address the combined future impact of sea level rise and intensified storms.
228. The project addresses the needs of the government of Ecuador, coastal communities, and important
economic sectors such as the shrimp and fisheries sector to address the risks and impacts of climate
change as described in the National Climate Change Strategy and the National Climate Change Plan.
D.5. Country ownership (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)
229. The project is fully aligned with national plans to combat climate change and with a set of national policies
and strategies. In addition to climate policies, the project is well aligned with a major new initiative
announced by the Government of Ecuador in March 2023 to protect its entire coastline and marine
resources within eight nautical miles of the coast. This initiative is in the early stages of design and
consultation, but the objective can’t be achieved without protection and sustainable management of the
country’s rich mangrove resources, which this GCF project will be helping to achieve.
230. The MAATE is the national environmental authority in charge of designing environmental policies and
coordinating strategies, programs, and projects for the conservation of ecosystems and the sustainable
use of natural resources. It proposes and defines the rules for environmental quality, with development
based on the conservation and adequate use of biodiversity and natural resources. The GCF Focal Point
(NDA) is part of the MAATE.
231. MAATE has been involved in the whole process of the development of the proposal, including development
of the PPF application, public consultations and review of drafts of the funding proposal. Several different
Under-secretariats of MAATE have been involved, including Climate Change, Natural Heritage and the
former Coastal and Marine Resources (now included within Natural Heritage).
232. The protection of mangroves in the context of climate risk management in coastal areas is included as a
priority in all current Ecuadorian climate policies, strategies and plans:
• National Climate Change Strategy 2012-2025: The proposed project directly addresses seven of the
eight national climate change priorities.
• NDC prioritizes addressing the multi-million-dollar cost of extreme climate events and recognizes
ecosystem conservation measures as important adaptation and mitigation measures.
• Mangroves are declared as forest and protective vegetation under Ecuadorian law and are an integral
part of the country's REDD+ efforts.

=== PAGE 69 ===
GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0 | PAGE 62
D
• In April 2017, the Organic Environmental Code was published in which there are 10 articles (Art. 99,
Art. 100, Art. 101, Art. 102, Art. 103, Art. 104, Art. 105, Art. 106, Art. 107, Art. 108) related to the
conservation of mangroves, among which it is established that mangroves (along with several other
categories of non-tree vegetation) are part of the National Forest Estate. Conservation of the
mangrove ecosystem is described according to Art. 99 of Chapter IV, which prohibits its affectation,
felling, and change of land use, in accordance with the law, and at the same time strengthens the
participation of the communities in the care of mangrove ecosystems.
• The National Adaptation Plan approved in February 2023 includes mangrove conservation and
restoration as an adaptation strategy.
233. Ecuador’s NDC does not provide detailed targets but highlights the following relevant mitigation actions
that the project will contribute to: conservation of natural heritage, strengthening of sustainable forest
management, strengthening of the restoration of natural heritage, strengthening forest control, and
strengthening the national system of PAs. The adaptation component of the NDC highlights the importance
of mangroves and the ecosystem functions that they provide. It identifies increasing the area of forests and
coastal ecosystems as a priority action to maintain their ecosystem function in climate change scenarios.
Further, the implementation of sustainable natural resource use is also highlighted as a priority. Finally, the
NDC notes as a priority the ‘creation and strengthening of capacities on climate change and natural heritage
management in social, academic, research and government sectors.’
234. The implementation plan for the first NDC (2020-2025) does not cover the proposed project period and an
implementation plan beyond 2025 has not yet been developed. However, the current 2020-2025
implementation plan provides a sense of level of ambition and how the project will contribute to achieving
NDC goals. The current plan includes a conditional goal of reducing GHG emissions from the Land Use,
Land-use Change and Forestry (LULUCF) sector of 62,074,310 MtCO2e. The estimated contribution of this
project to that goal would be 732,111 MtCO2e, or 1.2%, over the project implementation period, rising to
4,609,470 MtCO2e, or 7.4%, over the project lifespan. Other goals included in the NDC implementation
plan to which the project would contribute are: i) Update at least three tools for the management of PAs
with climate change adaptation criteria (four updated management plans, including adaptation measures,
are planned under the project); i) Increase the area under conservation by at least 127,500 ha through
Socio Bosque incentives to contribute to the maintenance of ecosystem functionality in climate change
scenarios (10,000 new hectares are planned under the project in addition to strengthening management at
60,000 hectares of existing AUSCEMs with incentive payments); and iii) Deliver at least 30 AUSCEM
agreements in mangrove areas.
235. Additional frameworks, such as the updated National Climate Change Plan, are still under development.
However, it is expected that this document will highlight the importance of coastal ecosystems for mitigation
and adaptation.
D.6. Efficiency and effectiveness (max`. 500 words, approximately 1 page)
236. The financial structure of the project will ensure efficient use of GCF funds by directing that funding at the
key barriers that are currently hindering progress to reduce mangrove deforestation and by encouraging
mangrove restoration efforts. The majority of the project funding is directed towards community-based and
public sector actions to strengthen mangrove conservation and restoration. The approximately 11% of GCF
funding used for activities with the private sector (i.e., Output 2.1) is aimed at small- and medium-sized
shrimp producers to provide training, promote the use of certification standards and to facilitate the access
to credit that these farmers need to invest in the CSS model and/or achieve certification SSP/ASC
standards. Investment on shrimp farms to implement more sustainable production methods will be financed
by the private sector and not using GCF funds. The GCF funds directed towards supporting the private
sector (~US$3.1 million) are expected to result in approximately US$5 million investment by the private
sector in climate-smart shrimp production methods and certification. Further uptake of these models
beyond the project implementation period is expected once these models are broadly recognized by the
industry to increase profitability and resilience as noted in section B5.
237. The overall efficiency of the Project with regards to expected mitigation benefits is extremely high with a
projected 4.6 MtCO2e of GHG emissions reduced and sequestered over the project lifespan, equivalent to

GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0 | PAGE 63
D
a cost of US$10 per tCO2e, which is in the typical range for avoided deforestation type mitigation projects.
In addition, the project will be bringing multiple adaptation benefits to a wide range of people, including
direct expected livelihood benefits for 41,500 people and reduced risk of flooding for 89,600 people. We
conservatively assume that the 41,500 people will also be beneficiaries of flood protection and therefore
use the figure of 89,600 people as the total direct beneficiaries for the project (i.e. we do not sum these two
figures together to get the overall number of beneficiaries), which equates to a cost of US$512 per person
over the six-year implementation period.
238. Grant funding is appropriate for the project because its primary goals are to secure public goods, in the
form of the ecosystem services that mangroves provide to achieve mitigation and adaptation benefits. Most
of the population that will benefit is defined as poor based on Ecuador’s unmet needs index and the direct
activities to improve livelihoods are directed at poor communities that can play an essential role in improving
mangrove management. The part of the project targeted at the private sector is designed to enable
increased private sector investment in climate-smart practices by addressing barriers related to lack of
knowledge and targeted support for the development of investment plans needed for unlocking further
private sector investment.
239. An Economic Analysis for the Project is provided as Annex 3a (model in Excel) and 3b (narrative) and
demonstrates the important economic value of the project. A Financial Analysis has not been included
because it is less meaningful for this project since its objective is to secure public goods and services rather
than generate a financial return. Based on the economic analysis assumptions, which were chosen to be
conservative, the economic Net Present Value (NPV) of the project over the 20 years and applying a 6%
discount rate is US$158 million more than the NPV for the ‘without project’ scenario. The estimated
economic benefits of the project outweigh the costs by a factor of 4.7. Further, the ‘project’ scenario as
presented in this Funding Proposal provided the most economic value of four scenarios that were
considered (based on the highest NPV, benefits to cost ratio and economic internal rate of return). The
three other alternative scenarios included in the economic cost benefit analysis were the ‘without project’
scenario, a scenario that only included the project’s proposed mangrove conservation activities to reduce
deforestation and a scenario that only included the project’s proposed mangrove restoration targets and
activities. The high economic value of the proposed project reflects the very high economic value of
mangroves to the broader economy of Ecuador. Much of the value is driven by the importance of fisheries
production, which underpins the livelihoods of the coastal fisher families that make up much of the
vulnerable population of coastal Ecuador. The sensitivity analysis included in the model shows that the
economic benefits of the project (and in fact all the scenarios considered) are robust to increases in costs
and reductions in benefits of +/- 30%. The model (Annex 3a) can be adjusted to consider the impacts of
larger changes.

environmental benefits, and knowledge
and financial barriers are removed, it can
be replicated within Ecuador, the region
and internationally.
The AUSCEM community management
model is already demonstrated but once
it is made more effective and linked to
sustainable community businesses, it
could be replicated throughout the
country and could be adopted in other
countries. | The project will address knowledge and
financial barriers to promote adoption of
the CSS model and more widespread
adherence to standards such as the
ASC’s and those advocated by Ecuador’s
SSP. The project will provide the
necessary knowledge, remove financial
barriers and provide demonstrations of
the model that will allow other companies
to replicate it both nationally and
internationally.
Further strengthening of the AUSCEM
community management model and
further demonstration of its benefits to
communities will lead to increased
demand for new AUSCEMs.
Sustainability | Government commitment to
improving mangrove conservation
is evidenced by recent decrees to
ensure that aquaculture
operations are operating legally,
as well as the operation and the
funding of four mangrove PAs
covering 64,916 ha. However,
legal requirements for shrimp
farms regarding mangrove
restoration and conservation are
low and significant areas of
mangroves remain outside the PA
network and community-managed
areas. | Low | The paradigm shift would see improved
decision making and enforcement of
existing legislation to protect mangroves
at all levels of government.
Improved enforcement of legislation
would be accompanied by the
widespread adoption of profitable,
climate-smart shrimp production models
linked to highly respected certification
standards that command a premium
price.
Financial institutions would encourage
investment in shrimp farming operations
that integrate climate-smart practices. | The project will strengthen the enabling
conditions necessary to sustain
reductions in the loss of mangroves by
strengthening governance, coastal
management policies and enforcement.
The project will be promoting the use of
improved climate-smart production
methods for shrimp farming. Once the
methodology of this profitable approach is
better known, existing private financing
will provide the means necessary for it to
be replicated.
The project will be expanding the Socio
Manglar subaccount (part of the existing
Socio Bosque Fund) and the government
will be seeking additional funding for it
from various sources to grow it over time

E.7. Monitoring, reporting and evaluation arrangements (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)
240. The Monitoring and Evaluation (M&E) Plan is provided as Annex 11. The plan includes GCF-level Impact and
Outcome indicators based on the IRMF and Project-level Outcome and Activity indicators, metrics for each
indicator, methodology for data collection, baseline information, frequency of data collection, and indicative
resources needed to complete the plan. The Project monitoring and evaluation will be coordinated by a
dedicated M&E specialist at CI-Ecuador in close coordination with subgrantees (notably PUCESE), partners
and other CI units contributing to the project. Project partners will be responsible for providing timely and
comprehensive reporting including technical results and financial data as necessary and appropriate. These
responsibilities will form part of contractual agreements between CI and the project partners and detailed
monitoring requirements will be established based on their roles. All monitoring data being collected by these
partners will be aggregated for project level reporting.
241. The Key Project Indicators, including measures of GHG emissions reductions and vulnerability reduction, as
outlined in sections E2 to E6 will be monitored by the Project’s M&E specialist (in coordination with staff at
partner organizations and with the aid of consultants where necessary) applying the methodologies and
frequencies as outlined in the Monitoring and Evaluation Plan (Annex 11). The M&E specialist will be assisted
by the project experts to be hired within CI and other CI units on gender and safeguards issues, blue carbon
and project impact evaluation.
242. The M&E Plan is a living document, follows the templates specified by GCF, and focuses on measuring the
project’s delivery of outputs, outcomes and broader paradigm shift. More detail will be added to the M&E Plan
during the inception stage of the project (the first six months of implementation) by the M&E specialist and in
collaboration with government agencies and local partners. Development of the full M&E Plan will ensure that
the M&E requirements are fully assimilated and that the plan is owned by the project staff. The full M&E plan
will build on the information provided in this document but elaborate in more detail on the roles and
responsibilities for data collection and management, information flows and reporting systems, finalized
indicators and means of verification, monitoring protocols and tools, implementation plans and schedules,
alignments and collaborations with existing national M&E systems. The detailed M&E Plan will also include
participatory methods for data collection and learning and an impact evaluation plan that builds on the summary
evaluation plan included in Annex 11.
243. The Project Steering Committee will meet annually to review and approve annual budget and workplans,
discuss implementation issues and identify solutions, and increase coordination and communication between
key project partners. The NDA will participate in these meetings as a member of the project steering committee.
244. CI-GCF Agency staff, as AE, will continuously monitor the work of the EE through quarterly EE reports and
meetings to monitor progress towards deliverables. CI-Ecuador, as EE, will prepare an Annual Performance
Report to monitor project progress and report to the CI-GCF Agency. The report will summarize the annual
project results and progress. CI-Ecuador will also provide a final report at the end of the Project.
245. The Project will undergo an independent Interim Evaluation in Year 4, consistent with GCF’s Evaluation Policy.
The evaluation will determine progress being made towards the achievement of outcomes and outputs and will
identify course correction if needed. The evaluation will incorporate an assessment of the Project’s
contributions to a paradigm shift in the enabling conditions using a three-point scale rating as specified in the
IRMF and with reference to the expected changes in enabling conditions noted in section E4 above.
Recommendations from the Interim Evaluation will be incorporated into the design of the Project to secure
maximum results and sustainability.
246. An independent Final Evaluation will be conducted at the end of the Project and will be undertaken in
accordance with the AE’s guidance and consistent with GCF’s Evaluation Policy. The Final Evaluation will
focus on the delivery of the Project’s results as initially planned (and as corrected after the Interim Evaluation,
if any such correction took place). Once again, it will include an assessment of the project’s contributions to a
paradigm shift in the enabling conditions using a three-point scale rating.

--- Table 1 on Page 87 ---
