E
RISK ASSESSMENT AND MANAGEMENT
F.1. Risk factors and mitigations measures (max. 3 pages)
Selected Risk Factor 1 A changing climate may impact the feasibility, sustainability or success of the project.
Category Probability Impact
Technical and operational High Low
Description
The base line study confirms that Ecuador’s coastal ecosystems are susceptible to climate change impacts. However,
the impact on the project activities themselves is considered limited. Moreover, it is one of the main aims of the project
to increase the resilience of the mangrove ecosystem to the impacts of climate change. The main climate change
impacts that may have a direct bearing on the project activities are those that could change the ability of mangroves to
survive such as SLR and physical changes to mangrove estuaries that could make some areas unsuitable for
mangrove. As noted in section B1, mangroves are dynamic systems and natural changes in mangrove cover can occur
as mud/substrate moves due to erosion or deposition. However, as also noted in section B1, projected SLR rates are
below the rates at which mangroves can adapt to the change by expanding into newly created suitable habitat.
Mitigation Measure(s)
Several aspects of the design of the project will address the potential threat that some areas will become less suitable
for mangroves over time due to climate change. First, the restoration approach that the project will follow will include
consideration of the hydrology of restoration sites to consider the current and future suitability of areas for mangrove
survival and growth. Second, the activities to develop adaptation plans for the PAs are intended to build resilience for
these areas, and mangroves in particular, by ensuring that potential impacts for each of the PAs are identified and
adaptation measures are integrated into management plans and current management. Finally, the work on PDOTs with
subnational governments includes land-use planning in which potential climate impacts will be identified and used to
identify appropriate land-use planning adaptations. For example, this would include identifying areas unsuitable for
development as they may be needed for mangrove ‘retreat’ under future SLR scenarios.
Selected Risk Factor 2 A changing climate may impact the adaptation opportunities provided by the project.
Category Probability Impact
Technical and operational Medium Low
Description
One climate change impact that could influence the adaptation opportunities provided by the project is SLR, as this
could influence the area available for reforestation. The base line study included an assessment of the vulnerability of
the entire project area to rising sea levels. SLR is expected in all parts of the project area, but the River Guayas Estuary
was identified as having the highest risk.
The vulnerability assessment included in the baseline study included the factor of SLR, particularly in the development
of maps prioritizing areas for mangrove reforestation. In fact, areas with high vulnerability to SLR (in combination with
other factors) were prioritized for reforestation. This is because experience with mangrove reforestation projects has
shown that successful reforestation can lead to increased sediments, allowing the young mangrove trees to form a
protective barrier against further climate change impacts, such as coastal erosion, and even protecting themselves
against future SLR (Spalding et al. 2014; Van Lavieren et al. 2012).
Mitigation Measure(s)
The project addresses this risk through Activity 1.1.1 under which stakeholder meetings will be conducted as part of
the detailed planning of restoration activities to record local knowledge on climate change impacts and potential
future impacts on mangrove reforestation, considering the views of men and women, for use in the final confirmation
of sites for reforestation, especially in the Guayas River Estuary.
Selected Risk Factor 3 There are risks to the project area from potential third parties’ action.

--- Table 1 on Page 89 ---
RISK ASSESSMENT AND MANAGEMENT |  | 
F.1. Risk factors and mitigations measures (max. 3 pages) |  | 
 |  | 
Selected Risk Factor 1 A changing climate may impact the feasibility, sustainability or success of the project. |  | 
Category | Probability | Impact
Technical and operational | High | Low
Description |  | 
The base line study confirms that Ecuador’s coastal ecosystems are susceptible to climate change impacts. However,
the impact on the project activities themselves is considered limited. Moreover, it is one of the main aims of the project
to increase the resilience of the mangrove ecosystem to the impacts of climate change. The main climate change
impacts that may have a direct bearing on the project activities are those that could change the ability of mangroves to
survive such as SLR and physical changes to mangrove estuaries that could make some areas unsuitable for
mangrove. As noted in section B1, mangroves are dynamic systems and natural changes in mangrove cover can occur
as mud/substrate moves due to erosion or deposition. However, as also noted in section B1, projected SLR rates are
below the rates at which mangroves can adapt to the change by expanding into newly created suitable habitat. |  | 
Mitigation Measure(s) |  | 
Several aspects of the design of the project will address the potential threat that some areas will become less suitable
for mangroves over time due to climate change. First, the restoration approach that the project will follow will include
consideration of the hydrology of restoration sites to consider the current and future suitability of areas for mangrove
survival and growth. Second, the activities to develop adaptation plans for the PAs are intended to build resilience for
these areas, and mangroves in particular, by ensuring that potential impacts for each of the PAs are identified and
adaptation measures are integrated into management plans and current management. Finally, the work on PDOTs with
subnational governments includes land-use planning in which potential climate impacts will be identified and used to
identify appropriate land-use planning adaptations. For example, this would include identifying areas unsuitable for
development as they may be needed for mangrove ‘retreat’ under future SLR scenarios. |  |

Selected Risk Factor 3 There are risks to the project area from potential third parties’ action. |  | 

=== PAGE 90 ===
GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0 | PAGE 83
E
Category Probability Impact
Technical and operational Medium Low
Description
The project area suffers from pollution from cities and their productive activities, agrochemicals, the proximity of large
ports and water contamination by ballast water. These forms of pollution could impact mangrove survival in parts of the
project area although they are likely to be very localized and involve small areas (with respect to the scale of the overall
project size). The potential impact is therefore considered to be low. These influences are largely outside of the control
of the project, at the same time, they further emphasize the need to increase the resilience of the mangrove ecosystem.
Mitigation Measure(s)
The project already addresses these issues:
• Activities 1.2.1, 2.1.1 and 2.1.2 aim to introduce climate-smart approaches to existing economic activities
or new bio-enterprises based on climate-smart approaches, which would both lead to less contamination
from harmful substances currently in use, e.g., in shrimp cultivation.
• Activity 3.2.1 includes work with local governments, municipalities and prefectures that are responsible for
territorial management and planning in the context of improved management of contamination.
Selected Risk Factor 4 Project staff could be at risk due to tensions between certain actors within the project
area that exist or may worsen as a result of project implementation.
Category Probability Impact
Technical and operational Medium Medium
Description
In the project area, there are tensions between shrimp farmers and fishermen, because some do not accept the existing
rules. The project aims to work especially with local associations, which may lead to a further divide between members
and non-members. Tensions between stakeholders within the project could disrupt activities if some stakeholders
decide they do not want to participate.
Furthermore, there is the risk of criminal gangs operating in remote mangrove areas. Ecuador’s remote coastal areas
are used to ship cocaine produced in neighboring countries further up the coast to Central America to the United States
and to Europe. The risk exists throughout the project area but is more acute in the northern estuaries and some areas
of the Guayas estuary. Violence between rival criminal gangs and with law enforcement is common, making it important
that project staff and partners operating in the mangroves are not mistaken as being from rival gangs or being
associated with law enforcement agencies.
Despite recent gang violence in Guayaquil, the risk to project staff as a result of existing tensions is considered to be
low and in case of actual security issues, CI’s Security and Protection Plan applies to CI staff as well as project partners.
CI already has a presence in the region, and there have been no gaps in operations, even with additional security
precautions that were put into place (i.e. no travel after dark). CI Ecuador benefits from strong and experienced
leadership, with a close relationship with the government and also with local partners, including communities where
this project will work. CI Ecuador has regional and office level Security Focal Points, which helps to identify and assess
risks quickly, efficiently, with the safety of CI, partners and beneficiaries the first priority. CI and partners also have
strong relationships with the communities, which not only makes it easier to operate during uncertain times and provides
CI with early warnings when conditions begin to deteriorate.
In addition, the project is likely to reduce existing tensions through its planned activities. The established project-specific
Grievance Redress Mechanism, if used by stakeholders as foreseen, can be used to track where tensions are
aggravated, and responses will be identified on a case-by-case basis to address these tensions.
Mitigation Measure(s)
For the potential risk of tensions between stakeholders: CI will implement the project-specific Grievance Redress
Mechanism to track grievances related to tensions between stakeholders and between stakeholders and the project
team. The team will initiate spaces and procedures for stakeholder dialogue where tensions between stakeholders and
the project team appear to have intensified as a result of the project and provide mediation to ensure improvement over
time. The project ESMP also includes the requirement to screen all the community grant support that will be provided

E
and consideration of tensions that the grant could create are to be assessed in the screening and mitigation measures
developed if the risks are considered medium or high level.
For the work with AUSCEM organizations, CI and PUCESE will also be encouraging AUSCEM organizations to include
more members to maximize participation of fishers within the associations and reduce the potential for conflict between
members and non-members.
To address safety risks posed by criminal activity, CI’s director for Safety and Security will conduct a thorough security
assessment and mitigation plan during project start-up. Example mitigation measures include pre-activity community
engagement to ensure local communities understand the project objectives and that there is no link to government
efforts to combat criminal gangs; Hostile Environment Awareness training and guidance on avoiding and reporting
criminal activity; and ensuring that it is easy to identify CI staff and partners as contributing to project objectives (rather
than criminal or law-enforcement activity).
An additional mitigation measure will be an emphasis on selection of consultants and subgrantees who have a
demonstrated history of operating in the project areas. PUCESE, the main project partner that will lead activities in the
two northern sites, is well known in the northern areas and familiar with working in those regions and with local
communities.
A Security Plan has been developed for this project and is included as Appendix 11 of the ESMP (Annex 6).
Selected Risk Factor 5 The project area is susceptible to earthquakes, subsidence, landslides, erosion, flooding
or extreme climatic conditions.
Category Probability Impact
Technical and operational High Low
Description
The project area is sensitive to erosion, flooding or extreme weather conditions, for example in El Niño or La Niña
years. These natural extreme events have the potential to disrupt project operations; however, the impacts on project
operations are expected to be low since the issues are likely to be relatively short-term by comparison to the length of
the project.
Mitigation Measure(s)
The project includes several activities that are mitigation measures themselves, i.e., they can jointly reduce the overall
impact of such extreme weather events or help improve the management of their impacts:
• Local people’s livelihoods will be improved through the development of new bio-enterprises (sub-activity 1.2.1);
and,
• The resilience and extent of the mangrove ecosystem will be increased, thus reducing the risk of impacts from
extreme weather events (sub-activity 1.1.1), specifically coastal flooding.
• Improve local planning, including identification of areas at risk of flooding and identification of priority natural
infrastructure to protect and restore.
Linked to the restoration activities (and related to risk factor 2 above), CI and PUCESE will consult with local
stakeholders to ensure their knowledge about the influence of extreme weather events is taken into consideration in
the establishment of bio-enterprises, if relevant (e.g. regarding the selection of sites to establish mangrove nurseries),
as well as in the final confirmation of sites for mangrove reforestation.
Selected Risk Factor 6 Political instability and changes in government agencies
Category Probability Impact
Governance Medium Low
Description
The political stability index has shown a steady improvement over the last decade and was at - 0.02 in 2014. The index
measures perceptions of the likelihood of Ecuador's government becoming destabilized.

E
Political risk in Ecuador is characterized by possible changes in government personnel in key positions or changing
mandates of different ministries because of elections. One example is that the administration of mangrove concessions
could be transferred from the MAATE to another Ministry, which could result in changes to concession regulations.
Since some of the Outcome 3 activities relate to working at the subnational governments, changes at those levels could
also impact the project operations. The main risk is that changes in personnel and/or government structures could
cause delays in project operations. Another example is that a new administration may seek to alter FIAS’s governing
structure or rules, which may impact its independence of operations. FIAS has taken several steps to enhance good
governance practices, including the expansion of its Board to provide for greater independence, consistent with best
practice standards for conservation trust funds. A risk remains that some of these practices could be changed under a
new administration.
A snap general election for both the executive and legislative branches took place in August 2023. All the leading
political parties and candidates embraced environmental issues as key pillars of their platform so it seems unlikely that
there would be backtracking on climate change commitments or support for mangrove conservation and restoration,
the key focus areas of this proposal. The regulatory frameworks and programs around which the proposed project is
built (Sustainable Use and Custody Agreements/AUSCEM and the Socio Manglar program) are enshrined in the
constitution and regulatory frameworks and are unlikely to be impacted. Although ministers may change following the
elections, ministerial level turn-over is not new for Ecuador and CI Ecuador has maintained strong relations at these
levels throughout numerous previous administrations and through many different ministers. Local and provincial officials
were not impacted by the 2023 elections, meaning that CI Ecuador’s strong relations with government at those levels
remained in place. Local and provincial elections will happen during the project implementation period but CI Ecuador’s
strong relations with those governments are expected to provide continuity for the project. A new administration has
been in place in Ecuador since December 2023 and the engagement of the government, local associations and private
sector remains.
Mitigation Measure(s)
CI has been able to maintain its activities during the last 20 years in the political context of Ecuador and has been able
to collaborate consistently with successive administrations and with subnational governments. Elections were held in
August 2023 and resulted in a presidential change in December 2023, however, not a change in the majority party.
All relevant ministries have been involved in the development of this project, including participation during Project
Preparation Funding activities of Funding Proposal development. The project has been designed to address
government priorities related to climate change, nature conservation and economic development and enjoys support
from the ministries involved. MAATE is even providing co-financing for the project. In the case of any changes in
mandate or personnel, CI will continue its extensive engagement with the government to ensure a smooth transition
and to address possible changes as needed.
The risk that a new administration would alter FIAS’s governing structure or rules is considered low risk and in the due
diligence there are some measures highlighted to design and implement before the granting process. There are internal
processes and policies that will be included in the grant to ensure compliance of use of GCF funds. The project budget
also includes support for capacity building and enforcement support to FIAS.
The risk presented by changing priorities and approaches from new administrations warrants monitoring, although it
has been reduced since FIAS’s inception due to concerted outreach by FIAS and engagement with the donor
community, resulting in reforms to FIAS’s governance. It is unlikely that there would be dramatic additional reforms to
FIAS so soon after the Government of Ecuador helped restructure it in 2017.
Of note, the vast majority of Fund decisions are made by an Administrative Board (“Junta Administrativa” or “JA”) for
each of FIAS’s subsidiary funds (such as the Socio Bosque Fund). These JAs were created during financing
agreements with donors, are largely determined according to the requirements in such agreements, and largely operate
independent of the FIAS Board of Directors. The governance structure of each of these JAs is defined in their governing
rules / operations manuals which may be subject to modification based on FIAS and donor agreements. The JAs are
primarily responsible for supporting the execution of projects, making decisions on fund allocations and disbursements,
consistent with the terms of any donor agreement. The JAs are responsible for the evaluation and oversight of donor

Selected Risk Factor 7 Prohibited Practices, including ML/FT
Category Probability Impact
Prohibited practices Low Medium
Description
The Project involves a wide array of actors, including government, sub-grantees, vendors and service providers, and
beneficiaries. Based on a calculation using multiple international risk indicators, CI assesses Ecuador to be a low-risk
country for GCF prohibited practices, including terrorist financing and money laundering. CI Ecuador follows and is
subject to CI‘s Anti-Money Laundering and Counter-Terrorism-Financing (AML/CFT) Policy and related procedures,
which have been assessed as effective by the GCF. In addition, CI-Ecuador is subject to national laws related to
AML/CFT, for which it has a country-specific Manual for Prevention of ML and FT. In compliance with the Organic
Regulation to the Law of Prevention and Detection of Laundering Money and Financing Terrorism, CI-Ecuador’s
AML/CFT practices are supervised by a public entity, the Financial Analysis Unit (Unidad de Análisis Financiero
[UAFE]), which conducts a thorough review of financial procedures related to AML/CFT and to which CI-Ecuador must
submit monthly reports. Finally, the project’s major non-EE recipients of GCF funds, sub-grantees PUCESE and FIAS,
were screened and cleared against international sanctions lists.
Concerning impact, serious breaches in prohibited practices, including ML/FT, would impact the reputation of CI and
the project.
Mitigation Measure(s)
CI has several procedures and controls in place to prevent, mitigate, and identify risk. In order to comply with CI
AML/CTF, U.S. law, and GCF fiduciary standards, CI applies rigorous CFT/AML screening to all CI funding recipients.
CI also conducts an assessment of Prohibited Practices (PP) risks of grant recipients and includes any required
mitigation measures in its legally binding grant agreements. All EE staff and subgrantees must also undergo training
on PP (including red flags and how to report violations), and all grant and services agreements (used by both CI and
sub-grantees) must include language on compliance with the GCF Policy on Prohibited Practices, including AML/CFT.
Sub-grantees PUCESE and FIAS will receive capacity-building related to AML/CFT and prohibited practices from CI
Ecuador, and compliance with PP policies and mitigation measures is part of project monitoring, including site visits
and CI audits, throughout implementation.
To report and remedy risks, any CI employee; employee of an executing entity, grantee, partner, or vendor; or
beneficiary may report a grievance or violation of these policies anonymously online or by telephone through CI’s
Ethics Hotline. All reported grievances will be investigated and addressed per CI’s Anti-Fraud Policy & Guidelines for
Investigations. Full details can be found on CI’s website at https://www.conservation.org/about/our-policies/reporting-
illegal-or-unethical-conduct-statement.
Selected Risk Factor 8 Unrealized or delayed co-financing
Category Probability Impact
Technical and operational Medium Low
Description
Co-financing plays an important part in achieving the full impact of the proposed project. There is a medium risk of
delayed or unrealized co-financing for the project, because MAATE’s grant co-financing depends on the approval
processes for government budgets. However, the committed co-financing is proposed for activity A.1.1.1 and therefore,
the negative impact to the project would be limited to this activity. In addition, a portion of the co-financing committed
by MAATE is ‘in-kind’ and related to staff salaries and therefore poses a low risk of not materializing.
Mitigation Measure(s)
The project team will periodically assess the degree of risk (both likelihood and impact) that co-financing will be funded
and its associated impact on project implementation. CI will also conduct regular engagement with co-financing sources
to understand and manage this risk. In the event that co-financing is not realized, CI will try to identify alternative
sources. If that is not possible, CI would work with GCF within the framework of the project’s FAA to propose adaptive

supported projects / funds. Accordingly, CI will ensure in its grant agreement with FIAS that the JA managing the Socio
Manglar subaccount meets best practices, including any GCF requirements. |  |

Category | Probability | Impact
Prohibited practices | Low | Medium
Description |  | 
The Project involves a wide array of actors, including government, sub-grantees, vendors and service providers, and
beneficiaries. Based on a calculation using multiple international risk indicators, CI assesses Ecuador to be a low-risk
country for GCF prohibited practices, including terrorist financing and money laundering. CI Ecuador follows and is
subject to CI‘s Anti-Money Laundering and Counter-Terrorism-Financing (AML/CFT) Policy and related procedures,
which have been assessed as effective by the GCF. In addition, CI-Ecuador is subject to national laws related to
AML/CFT, for which it has a country-specific Manual for Prevention of ML and FT. In compliance with the Organic
Regulation to the Law of Prevention and Detection of Laundering Money and Financing Terrorism, CI-Ecuador’s
AML/CFT practices are supervised by a public entity, the Financial Analysis Unit (Unidad de Análisis Financiero
[UAFE]), which conducts a thorough review of financial procedures related to AML/CFT and to which CI-Ecuador must
submit monthly reports. Finally, the project’s major non-EE recipients of GCF funds, sub-grantees PUCESE and FIAS,
were screened and cleared against international sanctions lists.
Concerning impact, serious breaches in prohibited practices, including ML/FT, would impact the reputation of CI and
the project. |  | 
Mitigation Measure(s) |  | 
CI has several procedures and controls in place to prevent, mitigate, and identify risk. In order to comply with CI
AML/CTF, U.S. law, and GCF fiduciary standards, CI applies rigorous CFT/AML screening to all CI funding recipients.
CI also conducts an assessment of Prohibited Practices (PP) risks of grant recipients and includes any required
mitigation measures in its legally binding grant agreements. All EE staff and subgrantees must also undergo training
on PP (including red flags and how to report violations), and all grant and services agreements (used by both CI and
sub-grantees) must include language on compliance with the GCF Policy on Prohibited Practices, including AML/CFT.
Sub-grantees PUCESE and FIAS will receive capacity-building related to AML/CFT and prohibited practices from CI
Ecuador, and compliance with PP policies and mitigation measures is part of project monitoring, including site visits
and CI audits, throughout implementation.
To report and remedy risks, any CI employee; employee of an executing entity, grantee, partner, or vendor; or
beneficiary may report a grievance or violation of these policies anonymously online or by telephone through CI’s
Ethics Hotline. All reported grievances will be investigated and addressed per CI’s Anti-Fraud Policy & Guidelines for
Investigations. Full details can be found on CI’s website at https://www.conservation.org/about/our-policies/reporting-
illegal-or-unethical-conduct-statement. |  | 
Selected Risk Factor 8 Unrealized or delayed co-financing |  | 
Category | Probability | Impact
Technical and operational | Medium | Low
Description |  | 
Co-financing plays an important part in achieving the full impact of the proposed project. There is a medium risk of
delayed or unrealized co-financing for the project, because MAATE’s grant co-financing depends on the approval
processes for government budgets. However, the committed co-financing is proposed for activity A.1.1.1 and therefore,
the negative impact to the project would be limited to this activity. In addition, a portion of the co-financing committed
by MAATE is ‘in-kind’ and related to staff salaries and therefore poses a low risk of not materializing. |  | 
Mitigation Measure(s) |  | 
The project team will periodically assess the degree of risk (both likelihood and impact) that co-financing will be funded
and its associated impact on project implementation. CI will also conduct regular engagement with co-financing sources
to understand and manage this risk. In the event that co-financing is not realized, CI will try to identify alternative
sources. If that is not possible, CI would work with GCF within the framework of the project’s FAA to propose adaptive |  |

management measures that may include reducing the scope of affected activities with minimal negative impact to
project objectives.
Selected Risk Factor 9 Risk of inflation
Category Probability Impact
Technical and operational Medium Low
Description
Although currently decreasing, Ecuador’s inflation rate for 2022 was 3.7% according to the International Monetary
Fund’s Data Mapper (April 2023). Ecuador’s average inflation rate over the period 2013 to 2022 was 1.8% with a
maximum of 4.2% in 201764. This period includes several turbulent years for Ecuador’s economy due to fluctuations in
the global price of oil, on which Ecuador’s economy is highly reliant. The IMF’s forecast for inflation in Ecuador is 1.34%
in 2025, rising to 1.54% in 2026 through to 202865. However, given the increases in inflation globally, the risk of rising
inflation is considered a ‘medium’ risk to this project as it could impact the budget, co-financing and activity
implementation. Higher than expected inflation would increase costs across the whole project, making it harder to
achieve all of the project activities. High inflation rates would be accompanied by high interest rates at financial
institutions, which could impact the likelihood of private sector shrimp farm companies taking loans to invest in climate
smart shrimp practices (Activity 2.1.2).
Mitigation Measure(s)
To mitigate for the impacts of inflation, CI has included inflation considerations into its budget figures. The budget has
been prepared assuming an inflation rate of 3%. The 3% figure is above the IMF’s forecast but given the challenges of
inflation forecasting and recent global increases in inflation, CI has used the 3% figure out of precaution. In addition, CI
will regularly monitor inflation pressures on the project budget and continually seek to identify cost savings where
possible.

65 https://www.theglobaleconomy.com/Ec uador/inflation_outlook_imf/; accessed 2 June 2023

GCF POLICIES AND STANDARDS
G.1. Environmental and social risk assessment (max. 750 words, approximately 1.5 pages)
248. The Environmental and Social risk screening for the project indicated that a limited and adequate Environmental
and Social Impact Assessment (ESIA) be conducted to identify potential project risks. This assessment resulted
in the following:
249. The project is risk category B (moderate risk).
250. An Environmental and Social Impact Assessment and Environmental and Social Management Plan (ESMP),
included as Annex 6, includes a summary of the project and the applicable legal and institutional framework,
the risks identified, the action plan with mitigation measures and implementation modalities. Also included are a
Grievance Redress Mechanism as well as:
• Natural Habitat Protection Plan;
• Process Framework for Restriction of Access to Natural Resources;
• Plan for Afro-descendant Communities and Cholos Pescadores;
• Stakeholder Engagement Plan (Annex 7); and
• Gender Action Plan (Annex 8).
251. The project does not intend to cause negative environmental or social impacts; on the contrary, it aims to
increase the resilience of the mangrove ecosystem and benefit local communities. It is based on rights-based
and inclusive approaches, including measures to ensure the participation of vulnerable groups, such as women,
youth and others.
252. Some risks are, by their nature, beyond the control of the project. For example, climate change may impact the
project area and there are localized impacts on the mangrove ecosystem from pollution in some places. The
project includes a variety of activities to address these issues.
253. Some details of activities will be defined during the project and therefore risk screening and appropriate
mitigation measures for these will be identified as part of the project activities. Screened activities that result in
a category B risk rating will require further limited ESIAs and ESMPs to be developed as per the guidance
provided in Annex 6. This applies to:
• Activities 1.2.1 and 1.2.2: It is necessary to identify which community livelihoods activities and small-scale
enterprises to be supported in various locations near the time of the work. Once the options and locations for
their development have been prioritized, their potential impacts must be assessed.
• Activities 1.1.1: Project preparation included the identification of areas with potential for reforestation. The
resulting map (see Annex 2) defines a potential of 10,000 ha while the project includes 4,850 ha as a target, so
the detailed selection of sites for reforestation is pending. To address this, detailed reforestation plans will be
developed as part of the reforestation activities including assessment of risks and measures to avoid
environmental damage and negative social impacts.
254. Apart from those mentioned above, the most important risks and their mitigation measures are:

G.2. Gender assessment and action plan (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)
255. During Project preparation, a gender assessment (see Annex 8) was undertaken to understand the differential
vulnerability to, and impact of, climate change on women and men in the project area. From the findings of this
assessment and an analysis of the proposed project design, a Gender Action Plan (GAP) was developed. The
process to produce these components included a desktop review and analysis of available literature and relevant
legislation, policy and programs in the country, as well as key informant interviews, household questionnaires,
focus group discussions and consultation workshops with project stakeholders and beneficiaries.
256. The GAP (see Annex 8) identifies opportunities to close gender gaps, reduce gender-based violence (GBV) and
the included actions are largely concentrated in the support of mangrove associations where direct collaboration
with the women and men who depend on, and manage, the mangroves will occur. These include mangrove use
agreements that integrate gender considerations, tailored and specific support to women in mangrove
associations (including the two women’s associations), setting targets (50/50) for women’s and men’s benefit
from the adoption of diversified and climate resilient livelihoods options linked to mangroves, and ensuring that
project staff and delivery partners are trained on gender equity principles and the importance of mitigating gender
inequalities in the project, including gender-based violence.
257. To support implementation of the GAP, CI will employ a full-time Gender Manager to ensure robust gender
analysis and lead the mainstreaming of gender into the project. The Gender Manager will develop and deliver
appropriate capacity building for colleagues and delivery partners that is well-designed, clear, tailored to the
context, and practical. The Gender Manager will accompany project teams to the field to ground truth progress
on gender outcomes: meet with women/women’s groups, collect qualitative information, help project teams to
problem solve, propose adaptive changes and participate in report preparation. The manager will support the four
estuaries and, in particular, the social coordinators in the implementation of the GAP. The manager will also
collaborate with CI’s global gender program to ensure consistency in gender approach across the institution and
will be supported by CI’s global Gender Advisor. The Gender Manager position will be based in Guayaquil city.
258. A complex set of historical, social, cultural, and economic factors influence the project sites’ current gender norms
and inequalities. There is a lack of political representation by women in decision-making, time deficit due to mostly
reproductive labor impinging on their ability to engage in community, organizational, and project activities, and
finally high rates of gender-based violence that further highlights inequalities and is an important barrier that

=== PAGE 97 ===
GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0 | PAGE 90
F
keeps women from exercising their voice and agency in mangrove conservation. This situation is compounded
by a number of structural problems associated with education, health, the provision of utilities and limited
employment opportunities.
259. In these mangrove-dependent communities, women are more vulnerable and experience higher rates of poverty
than men. Men tend to control the main economic activity in the communities: the harvesting and sale of seafood.
Often it is men who hold formal positions of leadership within communities and fishing associations.
260. There are distinct gender differences in mangrove use and management in the northern sites versus those in the
south. In the north, women walk (often collectively) to the mangroves and are therefore more actively engaged;
Mangrove associations in the north also have ~60% women members. The south is more male dominated;
women’s access and use of the mangrove areas is constrained by distance as the mangroves are far from the
community and must be accessed by boat (often individually). Because they are not familiar with the mangroves,
they are largely absent from the associations; mangrove associations in the south have ~15% women members.
261. Women living in mangroves work long days, which include family caregiving responsibilities, harvesting or fishing
activities, cooking for the family and looking after the children. The average working day for women is around 14
hours and little societal value is placed on their caregiving or reproductive tasks. By contrast, men have much
shorter days, fishing or harvesting for between four and five hours. This means that once their day’s fishing or
harvesting is over, they are relieved from work.
262. Rates of GBV are very high (67% in project sites) and could be perpetuated/increased by climate change impacts
that result in economic disruption.
263. As a result of gender norms, men and women are impacted differently by climate related events. Rising sea levels
and flooding – the two major effects cited in justifying climate change adaptation action in these project sites –
can also have consequences like an increase in water salinity and water-borne diseases, which are likely to affect
primarily women, who tend to be responsible for collecting water for the household and caring for sick family
members. Furthermore, existing gender inequalities may be exacerbated by the dynamics of climate change in
the project area.
G.3. Financial management and procurement (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)
264. The project will be executed by CI-Ecuador, and therefore, financial management and procurement for this project
will follow relevant CI policies and procedures, as well as relevant provisions in the Accreditation Master
Agreement (AMA) signed between CI and the GCF.
Financial management:
265. CI uses Unit4 Business World software suite as its enterprise resource planning (ERP) system for financial
accounting and monitoring in all its global operations. This system supports financial monitoring, reporting, human
resources, procurement, and record-keeping in line with the requirements of CI’s AMA. It supports a system of
ledger accounts to separate and track income and expenditure by donor, project, and subaward. It also provides
a coding system for monitoring and reporting of projects. In addition, all EEs and sub-grantees will be required to
submit quarterly financial reports that will be systematically reviewed by the AE and EE, respectively.
266. An annual audit of the project will be conducted by an independent external audit firm in accordance with audit
standards under U.S. GAAP and furnished to the GCF in accordance with CI’s AMA.
267. Disbursements made to CI will be recorded to the ledger account for this GCF project, which can be tracked using
the Business World system. Disbursements from the EE to sub-grantee will be made upon CI’s receipt of
acceptable progress and financial reports and any other requirements as included in the applicable grant
agreement.
268. CI has procedures and controls in place to prevent risk of prohibited practices, including ML/FT (collectively, ‘PP’).
CI applies rigorous CTF/AML screening to all CI funding recipients. CI also assesses PP risks of grant recipients
and includes mitigation measures in its grant agreements. All CI staff and subgrantees must also undergo training
on PP (including red flags and how to report violations), and all grant and services agreements (used by both CI
and sub-grantees) must include language on compliance with the GCF Policy on Prohibited Practices.
Compliance with PP policies and mitigation measures is part of project monitoring, including site visits and CI

shorter days, fishing or harvesting for between four and five hours. This means that once their day’s fishing or
harvesting is over, they are relieved from work.
262. Rates of GBV are very high (67% in project sites) and could be perpetuated/increased by climate change impacts
that result in economic disruption.
263. As a result of gender norms, men and women are impacted differently by climate related events. Rising sea levels
and flooding – the two major effects cited in justifying climate change adaptation action in these project sites –
can also have consequences like an increase in water salinity and water-borne diseases, which are likely to affect
primarily women, who tend to be responsible for collecting water for the household and caring for sick family
members. Furthermore, existing gender inequalities may be exacerbated by the dynamics of climate change in
the project area. |  | 
G.3. Financial management and procurement (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page) |  | 
 | 264. The project will be executed by CI-Ecuador, and therefore, financial management and procurement for this project | 
 | will follow relevant CI policies and procedures, as well as relevant provisions in the Accreditation Master | 
 | Agreement (AMA) signed between CI and the GCF. | 
 |  | 
 | Financial management: | 
 |  | 
 | 265. CI uses Unit4 Business World software suite as its enterprise resource planning (ERP) system for financial | 
 | accounting and monitoring in all its global operations. This system supports financial monitoring, reporting, human | 
 | resources, procurement, and record-keeping in line with the requirements of CI’s AMA. It supports a system of | 
 | ledger accounts to separate and track income and expenditure by donor, project, and subaward. It also provides | 
 | a coding system for monitoring and reporting of projects. In addition, all EEs and sub-grantees will be required to | 
 | submit quarterly financial reports that will be systematically reviewed by the AE and EE, respectively. | 
 |  | 
 | 266. An annual audit of the project will be conducted by an independent external audit firm in accordance with audit | 
 | standards under U.S. GAAP and furnished to the GCF in accordance with CI’s AMA. | 
 |  | 
 | 267. Disbursements made to CI will be recorded to the ledger account for this GCF project, which can be tracked using | 
 | the Business World system. Disbursements from the EE to sub-grantee will be made upon CI’s receipt of | 
 | acceptable progress and financial reports and any other requirements as included in the applicable grant | 
 | agreement. | 
 |  | 
 | 268. CI has procedures and controls in place to prevent risk of prohibited practices, including ML/FT (collectively, ‘PP’). | 
 | CI applies rigorous CTF/AML screening to all CI funding recipients. CI also assesses PP risks of grant recipients | 
 | and includes mitigation measures in its grant agreements. All CI staff and subgrantees must also undergo training | 
 | on PP (including red flags and how to report violations), and all grant and services agreements (used by both CI | 
 | and sub-grantees) must include language on compliance with the GCF Policy on Prohibited Practices. | 
 | Compliance with PP policies and mitigation measures is part of project monitoring, including site visits and CI | 

=== PAGE 98 ===
GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0 | PAGE 91
F
internal audits, throughout project implementation. Violations of PPs, fraud, or financial misuse may be reported
anonymously online or by telephone through CI’s Ethics Hotline. All reported grievances will be investigated and
addressed per CI’s Anti-Fraud Policy & Guidelines for Investigations.
269. Violations of Prohibited Practices, fraud, or financial misuse may be reported anonymously online or by telephone
through CI’s Ethics Hotline. All reported grievances will be investigated and addressed per CI’s Anti-Fraud Policy
& Guidelines for Investigations.
Procurement:
270. All procurement for this project must follow the Procurement Process Standards as defined in the CI Procurement
Policy, which requires that all procurements of goods or services ensure the best value and comply will all donor
funding terms and conditions, and that all goods be used only for their intended purposes and consistent with
CI’s Code of Ethics and suite of anti-fraud policies. CI’s procurement standards are in line with GCF fiduciary
standards.
271. All procurement done by the EE will follow CI’s internal procurement controls and risk-mitigation measures,
including procurement planning, multi-level purchasing approvals (including AE approval over a set threshold),
quarterly reporting on procurement & equipment, periodic inventories, and post-award reviews. CI’s Grant and
Contracts Unit must approve contracts of US$ 20,000 or more awarded by CI’s Ecuador office, including for this
project.
272. CI will assess the procurement policies of any subgrantee organization conducting procurement for this project.
Consistent with CI’s Procurement Policy, Project sub-grantees that meet CI’s Procurement Process Standards
as defined in the CI Procurement Policy will use with their own procurement policy and procedures for the project;
otherwise, they will be required to use CI’s Procurement Policy for the project in whole or in part. Detailed
procurement requirements consistent with CI and GCF standards will be included in the grant agreement signed
between CI and the grantee organization. The Project’s sub-grantee PUCESE will conduct procurement to
support the implementation of its Project activities, in line with these requirements and as described in Annexes
10 and 19.
273. CI will apply mitigation measures and monitor all procurement for this Project to ensure that any procured goods
are used only for their intended purposes. It will provide training on procurement and prohibited practices for CI
staff and subgrantees and will approve annual procurement plans and all procurements over a set threshold. It
will also conduct monitoring through quarterly reporting on procurement & equipment and through periodic site
visits.
274. An indicative plan for maintenance, operation, and disposition of GCF-funded equipment procured by CI
Ecuador is found in Annex 21.
G.4. Disclosure of funding proposal
No confidential information: The accredited entity confirms that the funding proposal, including its annexes, may be
disclosed in full by the GCF, as no information is being provided in confidence.
☒ With confidential information: The accredited entity declares that the funding proposal, including its annexes, may
not be disclosed in full by the GCF, as certain information is being provided in confidence. Accordingly, the accredited
entity is providing to the Secretariat the following two copies of the funding proposal, including all annexes:
• full copy for internal use of the GCF in which the confidential portions are marked accordingly, together with
an explanatory note regarding the said portions and the corresponding reason for confidentiality under the
accredited entity’s disclosure policy, and
• redacted copy for disclosure on the GCF website.
The funding proposal can only be processed upon receipt of the two copies above, if containing confidential
information.

Dear Madam,
We refer to the project titled Mangroves for climate: Public, Private and Community
Partnerships for Mitigation and Adaptation in Ecuador in Ecuador as included in the
funding proposal submitted by Conservation International Foundation to us on October 6th,
2023.
The undersigned is the duly authorized representative of Ministry of Environment, Water
and Ecological Transition, the National Designated Authority of Ecuador.
Pursuant to GCF decision B.08/10, the content of which we acknowledge to have reviewed,
we hereby communicate our no-objection to the project as included in the funding proposal.
By communicating our no-objection, it is implied that:
1. The government of Ecuador has no-objection to the project as included in the funding
proposal;
2. The project as included in the funding proposal is in conformity with the national
priorities, strategies and plans of Ecuador;
3. In accordance with the GCF’s environmental and social safeguards, the project as
included in the funding proposal is in conformity with relevant national laws and
regulations.
We also confirm that our national process for ascertaining no-objection to the project as
included in the funding proposal has been duly followed.
We acknowledge that this letter will be made publicly available on the GCF website.
Atentamente,

II. Summary of the Secretariat’s assessment
2 .1 Project background
3.
The project background, as set out in the funding proposal, underscores the pressing
need to address the impacts of climate change on Ecuador’s coastal regions. Climate change
projections indicate increasing risks, such as sea level rise, extreme weather events and coastal
flooding, that threaten local communities and economic activities. Mangroves play a crucial role
as natural barriers against erosion and flooding, yet ongoing deforestation exacerbates these
risks. Despite recent conservation efforts, mangrove loss continues, with adverse consequences
fo r carbon sequestration and biodiversity.
4.
The proposed interventions aim to address these challenges by engaging local
communities, the private sector and government entities in the protection and restoration of the
mangroves. Through effective mangrove management and restoration, the project seeks to
reduce greenhouse gas (GHG) emissions, to enhance flood protection for vulnerable coastal
populations and to improve livelihoods. It aligns with Ecuador’s national climate change
st rategies and contributes to global initiatives such as the Global Mangrove Alliance.
5.
The project’s components focus on increasing the area of mangroves under community-
based management, engaging the private sector in sustainable practices and strengthening
governance frameworks. By integrating these efforts, the project aims to achieve multiple
climate mitigation and adaptation goals, including reducing GHG emissions, protecting
ec osystems and enhancing infrastructure resilience.
6.
Moreover, the project’s alignment with the objectives of the Global Mangrove Alliance
underscores its potential for knowledge-sharing and global impact. By leveraging international
partnerships and best practices, the project aims to contribute significantly to global efforts to

7.
The project will directly benefit 89,600 individuals through enhanced access to climate
finance, adoption of climate-smart aquaculture practices, and mangrove restoration to mitigate
landslide and flooding risks. Moreover, the long-term conservation and sustainable
management of mangroves will indirectly benefit 3.4 million people, spanning 44 per cent of
coastal provinces and 20.1 per cent of the country’s population, as a result of the climate-
re silient practices.
8.
The total mitigation impact from mangrove conservation, restoration and preservation
amounts to an estimated GHG emission reduction of 732,111 tonnes of carbon dioxide
2 2

2

9.
The proposal includes a comprehensive budget totalling USD 45.9 million, with a
significant portion (USD 36.4 million) sought from the GCF as a grant. The remaining USD 9.5
million is anticipated to be sourced from co-financing by the Ministry of Environment, Water
and Transition of Ecuador and the Conservation International Foundation (CI), in the form of
both in-kind contributions and grants.
2Co.2mponCenotm 1:p Moannegrnotv-eb ayre-caso umndpeor nefefenctti vaen aanldy csliism ate-adapted management increased,
including through community-based management (AUSCEMs), and with protected areas
implementing climate adaptation plans (total cost: USD 26 million; GCF cost: USD 19.5 million)
10.
Component 1 of the project aims to increase mangrove areas under effective and
climate-adapted management (AUSCEMs), including through community-based management
and protected areas implementing climate adaptation plans. Despite the effectiveness of
community-based stewardship in protecting mangroves, there are gaps in coverage that can be
addressed through outreach and providing assistance in organizing, planning and strengthening
relationships with government programmes that recognize AUSCEMs. The component is crucial
to ensure that existing AUSCEM associations have the organizational capacity to sustain their
ste wardship efforts.
11.
Community-based mechanisms for mangrove conservation are often economically
precarious; therefore, generating stronger local economic benefits based on mangrove
conservation is essential for ensuring sustainability. Restoration efforts under community
ste wardship will contribute to enhancing mangrove coverage and associated benefits.
12.
To achieve these goals, output 1.1 focuses on reducing flood risk for vulnerable
populations and reducing GHG emissions, through strengthening community-based mangrove
management. Activities include providing training, technical assistance and equipment to
AUSCEM groups, supporting the expansion of AUSCEMs, and assisting with the application
pr ocess for incentives under the country’s Socio Manglar (SM) programme.
13.
Output 1.2 aims to improve livelihood activities and economically productive
community businesses to incentivize participation in mangrove conservation and restoration.
This involves providing technical and business development support to mangrove community
associations, particularly focusing on women, youth and other vulnerable groups. In addition,
financial mechanisms will be established and consolidated to support micro- and small-scale

enterprises, including cash grants and assistance in accessing credit and investment from
ex isting financial institutions.
14.
Overall, these efforts seek to strengthen community-based mangrove conservation and
management, reduce deforestation, increase economic benefits for local communities and
Component 2: The private sector becomes a transformational agent for change by reducing GHG
enhance resilience to climate change.
emissions and providing financial support to conserve and restore mangroves that increase
climate resilience for other coastal populations (total cost: USD 10.2 million; GCF cost: USD 8.9
million)
15.
The project aims to engage the private sector in promoting improved shrimp production
methods to reduce mangrove deforestation and advance mangrove conservation and
restoration in Ecuador. CI is the accredited entity (AE), and it will collaborate with Ecuador’s
National Conservation Agency to provide training on sustainable shrimp production (SSP) and
promote climate-smart shrimp (CSS) farming methods, including mangrove restoration on
farms. CI will also engage consultants to help small- and medium-sized shrimp farms access
pr ivate finance.
16.
Seed funding will be provided by the Aquaculture Stewardship Council and CI to
establish a subaccount within Ecuador’s Socio Bosque Fund to support the SM programme for
the incentive payment. The project aims to catalyse investment in CSS farming methods, leading
to reduced GHG emissions, increased profitability and mangrove restoration.
17.
Technical assistance will be provided to shrimp farms for adopting SSP and CSS
standards, with a focus on sustainable intensification and mangrove restoration. The project
will facilitate partnerships and access to finance for shrimp farms transitioning to sustainable
practices, including through the Eco Business Fund. It will also establish agreements with
bu sinesses to contribute to mangrove conservation and to finance the SM programme.
18.
In addition, the project will disseminate knowledge through various platforms and
workshops, develop a marketing strategy for CSS farming, and seek to mobilize private
investment into the SM subaccount, supported by funds from the GCF. These efforts aim to
strengthen private sector engagement in mangrove conservation and restoration while
promoting SSP practices in Ecuador. With successful management, the SM subaccount is
expected to create a long-term financial mechanism that attracts private sector investment and
Component 3: Create the enabling conditions for sustaining reductions in mangrove deforestation
provides incentive payments for sustainable mangrove conservation and restoration.
and increased mangrove restoration by strengthening governance, climate change adaptation
strategies, coastal management policies and legal enforcement (total cost: USD 6.2 million; GCF
cost: USD 5.5 million)
19.
Actions by local communities and the private sector to protect mangroves and maintain
flood protection benefits as a nature-based climate change adaptation and mitigation strategy
are contingent upon the Government of Ecuador establishing the necessary governance,
regulatory and enabling conditions. These conditions are essential for deterring and controlling
illegal activities and ensuring that climate considerations are integrated into planning and
inv estment decisions.
20.
By providing better climate change and mangrove forest information to decision-makers
and stakeholders, particularly focusing on public entities, the project will directly contribute to
increasing climate knowledge for decision-making, laying the groundwork for achieving project
ou tcomes related to mangrove management and climate resilience.
21.
Improved legal and regulatory capacity, focusing on planning, preparedness and
enforcement, will support sustaining mangroves and enhancing awareness and resilience

GCF/B.39/02/Add. 10
Page 110
among beneficiaries, primarily in the eight priority municipalities of the project area. This will
directly contribute to strengthening institutional and regulatory systems for climate-responsive
pla nning and development.
22.
To achieve these objectives, the project includes several activities under output 3.1, such
as monitoring mangrove conditions and socioeconomic impacts, integrating remote sensing and
community participation for emissions reduction monitoring, and conducting socioeconomic
impact evaluations. Output 3.2 entails harmonizing legal and regulatory frameworks at the local
and sectoral level, incorporating climate resilience and mitigation strategies, and enforcing
reg ulations with a focus on human rights.
23.
Activities under output 3.2 include supporting local governments to improve planning
instruments that integrate climate change adaptation and mangrove management,
strengthening regulatory frameworks and law enforcement by relevant agencies, and
generating proposals for regulatory changes in various sectors. This will involve analysing legal
frameworks, facilitating discussions on legal reforms and providing training to judiciary staff. In
addition, the project will develop an online and mobile communications application (app) for
reporting deforestation and other impacts on mangroves, thereby enhancing transparency and
enforcement efforts.

24.

indirect beneficiaries. Furthermore, it targets an estimated GHG emission reduction of
4,6 09,470 t CO
2

25.
The AUSCEM project seeks to address both adaptation and mitigation challenges posed
by climate change, particularly in vulnerable coastal regions. By leveraging the protective
capacities of mangrove ecosystems, the project endeavours to yield significant benefits for both
local communities and the global environment. This project represents a multifaceted approach
to addressing climate change impacts at both the local and the global scale, highlighting the
crucial role of mangrove ecosystems in fostering resilience and mitigating environmental
degradation. The project offers both adaptation and mitigation benefits, focusing on reducing

Scale: N/A
3. 2 Paradigm shift potential
28.
This project aims to transform Ecuador’s approach to valuing and utilizing critical
natural infrastructure, particularly mangrove ecosystems, to protect coastal communities from
flooding. By integrating the adaptation and mitigation benefits of mangroves into governance
and economic frameworks, the project offers an innovative solution to reducing GHG emissions
and enhancing the livelihoods of vulnerable populations. It addresses the current
undervaluation of climate risks and ecosystem services provided by mangroves, which is the
res ult of inadequate information, institutional coordination and private sector investment.
29.
The project employs a multifaceted strategy, including strengthening public sector
frameworks, fostering public–private partnerships and empowering local communities, with a
focus on leveraging the “green infrastructure” of mangroves. This approach is cost-effective and
scalable, and includes lessons, guidance and tools that can be used for other coastal
communities threatened by climate change. Comprehensive monitoring and reporting
mechanisms will facilitate learning and knowledge-sharing throughout the project, with mid-
ter m and final evaluations to identify lessons learned and disseminate findings.
30.
The project aims to create an enabling environment and policy framework by
strengthening regulatory frameworks and fostering dialogue and collaboration across sectors at
the national and subnational level. Capacity-building initiatives will empower local communities
to participate in innovative business models and to engage in land-use planning processes with
local government. The project emphasizes consensus-building among stakeholders through
participatory processes, facilitating agreements for the sustainable management of mangroves.
Private sector participation will be enhanced through support for land-use planning and the
ad option of climate-smart practices, particularly mangrove conservation and restoration.
31.
The project focuses on scaling up successful mangrove conservation and restoration
activities in Ecuador, addressing the lack of basic land-use planning instruments in the project
area. Participatory planning processes will be facilitated, documented and shared with the
national government, with recommendations for legal and regulatory adjustments as needed.
Scale: N/A
3. 3 SEnuvsitraoinnmaebnleta dl ceov-beelonpefmitse: nt potential
32.
The project aims to reduce deforestation, enhance
restoration and improve management practices across Ecuador’s entire mangrove area, which
spans 156,633 hectares. In addition to mitigating climate change and reducing flood risks,
mangroves offer further ecosystem services; for example, they support biodiversity
conservation, acting as habitats for numerous plant and bird species. Mangroves also serve as
vital nursing grounds for fisheries, fostering the spawning, breeding and early development of
various fish species. Thus mangroves play a significant role in fish production and provide
substantial economic value through fisheries-related ecosystem services. The total ecosystem
service value of mangroves, including biodiversity and other services, is estimated to be
substantial, providing essential benefits to local economies.

environmental considerations, including water management and waste disposal,
benefiting human health and well-being; and
(c)
Collaborative efforts to control illegal activities in mangrove areas between community-
based associations and government authorities will enhance local security, generating

36.

Scale: N/A
3. 4 Needs of the recipient
37.
Ecuador has experienced notable economic growth from 2007 and 2018, resulting in a
reduction in the levels of poverty and inequality and the ongoing expansion of the middle class.
However, the economy’s heavy reliance on the oil sector presents a significant vulnerability,
pa rticularly evident during periods of low oil prices and the appreciation of the US dollar.
38.
With over 3,630 km of coastline, Ecuador’s five coastal provinces are home to
approximately 54 per cent of the population. These areas face significant risks from sea level
rise, saltwater intrusion, storm surges, flooding and storms. Coastal regions of Ecuador are
disproportionately affected by poverty, with 45 per cent of the population in the project area
living below the extreme poverty line. Unsafe housing conditions persist due to rapid
ur banization and persistent poverty.
39.
The challenges of coastal development are compounded by the threats of climate
change, yet Ecuador lacks an integrated plan to protect mangroves and wetlands from the
combined impacts of sea level rise and intensified storms. The project addresses these pressing
needs by aligning with Ecuador’s national climate change strategy and plan, with the aim of
supporting the national and local government, coastal communities and crucial economic
sectors such as shrimp farming and fisheries.

=== PAGE 113 ===
GCF/B.39/02/Add. 10
Page 113
Scale: N/A
3. 5 Country ownership
40.
The project is in alignment with the NDC in terms of contributing to both mitigation and
adaptation goals through the aim of protecting mangrove ecosystems. The project is also aligned
to other national policies, including the national climate change strategy, the conservation of the
national forest estate, and REDD+ efforts. Moreover, the project is linked to the national
measurement, reporting and verification and monitoring and evaluation (M&E) systems for
NDC implementation; thus the results will be aggregated into the national biennial transparency
reports that the national government will submit under the United Nations Framework
Co nvention on Climate Change.
41.
In addition, country ownership is firmly established through active government
participation in project management structures. Both the Ministry of Environment, Water and
Ecological Transition and the Vice-ministry of Aquaculture and Fisheries will be part of the
project management advisory committee and the technical committees guiding each project
component. This involvement enhances ownership and promotes awareness of the
technological solutions proposed by the project, setting the foundation for replication and
scalability in the future.
Scale: N/A
3. 6 Efficiency and effectiveness
42.
The project provides the most value of the scenarios considered over both the project
implementation period (6.5 years) and the project lifespan (20 years), and it gives a high
benefit-to-cost ratio (8:1). The project is expected to generate USD 299 million of economic
value over and above the “without project” scenario over the 20-year project lifespan. As such,
the project as designed provides excellent value for money because it will generate significant
benefits to society over both the implementation period (6.5 years) and the expected project
lifespan (20 years).
IV. Assessment of consistency with GCF safeguards and policies
4. 1 Environmental and social safeguards
43.
The project aims to achieve GHG emission reductions while providing flood protection
and enhancing the livelihoods of the communities through coordinated efforts to protect and
restore the mangrove ecosystem in Ecuador. The project interventions are intended to have
positive impacts on people and the environment. Any unintended adverse impacts are expected
to be minor and site-specific, and can be managed through careful design and standard
mitigation measures. The environmental co-benefits due to reduced deforestation, restoration
and management of the mangrove ecosystem include enhanced habitats, particularly for bird
and fish species, and enhanced biodiversity and ecosystem services. The social co-benefits
include reduced flood risks, improved capacity for mangrove management and better human

44.
The project has

are several and located in multiple sites involving various communities and development

assessment and an environmental and social management plan (ESMP) which together identify
the potential risks and impacts and possible mitigation measures and set out the procedures for
assessing and managing E&S risks and impacts of the individual interventions (subprojects).

areas and buffer zones. The project is not expected to result in the introduction of invasive

. The project will have a project steering committee

58.
The project has described a project-level GRM
that will be promoted during the awareness-raising and capacity-building activities of the
project. It describes the types of complaints that can be addressed, who can raise a complaint,
the contact points at the community level, and the responsible authorities in handling
complaints. The GRM also describes the ability for stakeholders to access CI’s Ethics Hotline that
employees, grantees and other partners and beneficiaries can access to report incidents
anonymously. The GRM also allows for stakeholders to access the GCF’s Independent Redress
Mechanism. In line with the GCF Indigenous Peoples Policy, the GCF Indigenous Peoples Focal
Point will be available for assistance at any stage, including before a claim has been made.
4. 2 Gender policy
59.
The AE provided a gender assessment and action plan with the funding proposal, and
therefore complies with the requirements of the GCF Gender Policy. The assessment report
presents information on the differential impact of and vulnerability to climate change for
women and men in the targeted project area. The gender assessment is based on secondary
data, as well as consultation workshops that were held between 2016 and 2019. However, the
majority of the secondary data is from outdated sources (i.e. 2010–2012). The Secretariat
therefore recommends that, during early project implementation, the AE may need to make
some revisions to the documents in annex 8 of the funding proposal if there are any major
changes in the demographics and/or socioeconomic statuses that may have a bearing on this
sp ecific project.
60.
The gender action plan for the project outlines various interventions in order to ensure
that anticipated gender outcomes are realized. The emphasis is on interventions that will aim at
increasing women’s participation in ensuring diversified and climate-resilient livelihood
options in mangroves, as well as increasing women’s leadership, skills building within
associations and gender quotas, among other things. The Secretariat recommends and suggests
that the AE include the following provisions: (i) engage local women and fisherwomen in
participatory project planning and implementation, including utilizing their knowledge of
marine and coastal resources in assessments, and their traditional practices in natural resource
management; (ii) ensure that women-led businesses have improved financial and market access
and profitability, and address barriers within families and households by involving men; (iii)
build critical awareness among women and disadvantaged groups on their rights as women and
fisherfolk; and (iv) define common goals and develop strategic alliances between women’s
groups and other organizations/associations for more effective advocacy for policy change.
Gender balance should be considered when forming stakeholder groups; this may involve
including female minority leaders and managers to encourage participation and capacity-
building. Also, the AE needs to ensure that financial leakage is eliminated and that financial
resources actually reach the needy, poor, vulnerable and disadvantaged communities including
women and girls.

to protect and restore mangroves in Ecuador. This initiative plans to reduce mangrove loss,

: The Secretariat notes that there are reports of threats from criminal

particularly Guayaquil, are reportedly entangled in the cocaine trade, with drug traffickers

security concerns in the country, potentially disrupting project implementation. The AE also
acknowledges these challenges posed by criminal gangs operating in remote mangrove areas
and the exploitation of Ecuador’s coastal areas for cocaine trafficking, underscoring potential
impacts on project activities. In its mitigation plan, the AE has developed a security plan (annex
6, ESMP appendix 11), and CI’s Safety and Security Director will conduct a security assessment
and develop a mitigation plan during project startup. The mitigation measures will include pre-
activity community engagement, hostile environment awareness training and ensuring easy

65.
The use of grants (i.e. 100 per cent
concessionality) is justified given the country context where public and private funding for
mangrove conservation and restoration efforts is limited and insufficient. Vulnerable
populations in Ecuador are directly exposed to heightened flood risks exacerbated by climate
change, and they lack adaptive capacity due to poverty. Grant funding is therefore warranted to
create the enabling conditions for sustaining capacity and incentivizing private sector
investments in mangroves, ensuring the integration of climate resilience measures and fostering
long-term sustainability.

4.3.4. Compliance risk (medium risk)
66.
Ecuador is not currently subject to United Nations Security Council resolutions, nor is it
currently identified on the Financial Action Task Force lists of high-risk jurisdictions or
jurisdictions under increased monitoring relating to anti-money laundering (AML) and
co untering the financing of terrorism (CFT).
67.
The funding proposal noted a risk of criminal gangs operating in project areas and rated
the probability as “medium”. The mitigating measures described in the security plan include
staff training, mapping of high-risk areas, and a country threat and vulnerability assessment, as
well as cooperation with local partners and authorities. The funding proposal also noted that
project areas are located along a drug shipping route. The AE provided further information on
its AML/CFT controls in annex 20 to the funding proposal and confirmed that its AML/CFT
po licies include sanctions screening of subgrantees and EE (CI Ecuador) employees.
68.
Furthermore, as the political and security situation in Ecuador has evolved since the
proposal was first submitted, the AE confirmed that the security plan has been updated
acc ordingly, with additional security costs including in the budget.
69.
Given the aforementioned risks, as well as the ongoing uncertainty within Ecuador, this

4. 5 Results monitoring and reporting
78.
As a cross-cutting project, the funding proposal aims to achieve a combination of
mitigation and adaptation outcomes spanning three GCF results areas: forestry and land use for
mitigation; most vulnerable people and communities, and ecosystems and ecosystems services
for adaptation. The expected mitigation outcome is an estimated GHG emission reduction of
2 2
4,609,470 t CO eq over the project’s 20-year lifespan and 732,111 t CO eq by the end of the
implementation period. The expected adaptation beneficiaries amount to 89,600 and 3.4 million
direct and indirect beneficiaries respectively. The AE submitted the necessary annex to
de monstrate the calculation methods used for these estimates.
79.
The theory of change has been improved and refined to clearly articulate the results
chain that connects the proposed activities, outputs, outcomes and impact. It covers pathways
tha t will lead to the expected climate outcomes and co-benefits.
80.
The logical framework is consistent with the requirements of the Integrated Results
Management Framework. The project is expected to realize its paradigm shift potential through
a combination of building community knowledge and resources for mangrove management and
livelihoods, strengthening the enabling environment for mangrove management, and
strengthening collaboration between the private sector and government on mangrove
protection and restoration. Core indicator 2 (adaptation beneficiaries), supplementary indicator
2.1 (adoption of climate-resilient livelihood options), supplementary indicator 3.1 (change in
expected losses), core indicator 4 (natural resources brought under improved management
practices), supplementary indicator 4.1(natural resources restored) and core indicator 1 (GHG
emissions reduced) have all been incorporated into the logical framework and relevant means
of verification, targets and assumptions have been specified.
81.
The M&E plan, which includes indicator-specific approaches for data collection and data
sources, and an evaluation plan, are well provided for in the project budget and AE fees
respectively. The budget allocation for the M&E activities is consistent with the requirements of
the GCF Evaluation Policy.
4. 6 Legal assessment
82.
The AMA was signed with the AE on 13 July 2017, and was amended and restated on 15
Au gust 2022, which became effective on 17 August 2022, for the second accreditation term.
83.
The AE has provided a legal certificate confirming that it has obtained all internal
approvals, and it has the capacity and authority to implement the project pursuant to clause
4.1 3 of the AMA.
84.
The proposed project will be implemented in Ecuador, a country in which GCF is not
provided with privileges and immunities. This means that, among other things, GCF is not
protected against litigation or expropriation in this country, which risks need to be further

=== PAGE 120 ===
GCF/B.39/02/Add. 10
Page 120
assessed. The GCF Secretariat provided a draft agreement on privileges and immunities and a
ba ckground note to the Government of the Republic of Ecuador on 23 February 2023.
85.
It is recommended that any approval by the Board is made subject to the following
co nditions:
(a)

GCF Secretariat within 180 days from the date of Board approval; and
(b)
Completion of the legal due diligence to the satisfaction of the GCF Secretariat.

Independent Technical Advisory Panel’s assessment of
FP235
Proposal name: Mangroves for climate: Public, Private and Community
Partnerships for Mitigation and Adaptation in Ecuador
Accredited entity: Conservation International Foundation
Country: Ecuador
Project/programme size: Small
I. Assessment of the independent Technical Advisory Panel
1 .1 Overview
1.
The Mangroves for climate: Public, Private and Community Partnerships for Mitigation
and Adaptation in Ecuador project is a cross-cutting project aimed at strengthening the
resilience of Ecuador’s coastal areas to the negative impacts of climate change, while also
significantly reducing greenhouse gas (GHG) emissions. Over an implementation period of six-
and-a-half years, this small-sized project will bring together the public, private, and community
sectors to focus on the conservation and restoration of mangrove ecosystems, which are crucial
for coastal protection, biodiversity, and local livelihoods. This project meets the urgent need for
climate adaptation and mitigation in regions prone to extreme weather and rising sea levels,
pr oviding direct benefits to Ecuador’s most vulnerable coastal communities.
2.
